ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS
The funding for Australia’s participation in TIMSS 2023 was provided jointly by the Australian Government 
and the state and territory governments. All of Australia’s share of the international overheads and half of 
the basic funding for TIMSS within Australia was supported by the Australian Government Department of 
Education, while each state and territory government education department provided funding in proportion to 
the size of its student population.
In Australia, TIMSS is managed by a team from the Australian Council for Educational Research (ACER) 
and guided by the International Assessments Joint National Advisory Committee (IAJNAC). ACER wishes 
to thank the IAJNAC members for their continued interest and commitment throughout every phase of 
the project. Their involvement included assisting with the implementation of TIMSS in schools in their 
jurisdiction, reviewing the report and providing valuable information to ensure the continued success of 
TIMSS in Australia.
The undertaking of TIMSS 2023 was a collaborative effort. A national survey such as TIMSS could not be 
successful without the cooperation of the school systems, principals, teachers, parents and, of course, 
the students. A high participation rate of the randomly selected schools and students is essential for the 
assessment to obtain high-quality data. It is thanks to this level of cooperation that Australia was able to 
satisfy the internationally set response criteria fully for TIMSS 2023. ACER gratefully acknowledges the 
assistance of education system officials Australia-wide, and the principals, teachers, parents and students in 
the participating schools who so generously gave their time and support in contributing to the project.
Parts of this report are reproduced and/or modified, with permission, from the TIMSS 2023 international 
results in mathematics and science (von Davier et al., 2024) report and the TIMSS 2023 assessment 
frameworks (Mullis, et al., 2021).

xii
xii
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
The Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) is an international comparative study of 
student achievement directed by the International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement 
(IEA). TIMSS was first conducted in 1995 and the 2023 assessment formed the eighth cycle, providing 
28 years of trends in mathematics and science achievement at Year 4 and Year 8. In Australia, TIMSS is 
managed by the Australian Council for Educational Research (ACER) and is jointly funded by the Australian 
Government and the state and territory governments. TIMSS is a key part of Australia’s National Assessment 
Program.
The goal of TIMSS is to provide comparative information about educational achievement across countries to 
improve teaching and learning in mathematics and science. TIMSS is based on a research model that uses 
the curriculum, within context, as its foundation. TIMSS is designed, broadly, to align with the mathematics 
and science curricula used in the participating education systems and countries, and focuses on 
assessment at Year 4 and Year 8. TIMSS also provides important data about students’ contexts for learning 
mathematics and science based on questionnaires completed by students and their parents, teachers and 
school principals.
The 2023 cycle of TIMSS completed its transition to a digital assessment; this was the first time it was 
offered as a completely online assessment. TIMSS 2023 also expanded its scope to assess environmental 
knowledge and attitudes. This new component provides valuable insights into students’ understanding of 
environmental issues, including climate change, biodiversity, and conservation.
This report presents the results for Australia as a whole, for the Australian states and territories and for 
other demographic groups in TIMSS 2023. The results can be viewed in an international context, and student 
performance can be monitored over time. The results also allow for nationally comparable reports of student 
outcomes against the Alice Springs (Mparntwe) education declaration (Education Council, 2019).
Who is assessed?
TIMSS assesses a random sample of Year 4 and/or Year 8 students, drawn from a nationally representative 
sample of schools. Their principals and mathematics and science teachers are also asked to complete a 
survey.
In TIMSS 2023, 58 countries participated at Year 4 and 44 countries participated at Year 8. In total, over 
650,000 students participated worldwide.
In Australia, 283 primary schools and 276 secondary schools participated in the data collection for TIMSS 
2023. At least one intact class from the relevant year level was selected from each school, resulting in a 
sample of 5,470 Year 4 students and 8,442 Year 8 students. 

Executive summary
xiii
What is assessed?
TIMSS is organised around 2 dimensions: a content dimension, which specifies the domains or subject 
matter to be assessed in mathematics and science, and a cognitive dimension, which specifies the thinking 
processes and sets of behaviours expected of students as they engage with the content.
At Year 4, there are 3 content domains in mathematics: Number, Measurement and geometry, and Data; and 
3 in science: Life science, Physical science and Earth science.
At Year 8, there are 4 content domains in mathematics: Number, Algebra, Geometry and measurement, and 
Data and probability; and 4 in science:  Biology, Chemistry, Physics and Earth science.
At both year levels, there are 3 cognitive domains in each curriculum area: Knowing, Applying and Reasoning.
How are the results reported?
Results are reported as mean (average) scores, as distributions of scores and as percentages of students 
who attain the international benchmarks, for countries and for specific groups of students within Australia.
Each of the international benchmarks (Low, Intermediate, High and Advanced) contains descriptions of the 
skills typically shown by students achieving at each level, as defined by international experts. Students who 
achieved the Advanced international benchmark are considered to be highly proficient in mathematics and 
science and are considered to be very high performers; students who performed below the Low international 
benchmark struggled to display even basic mathematical or science knowledge and understanding, and 
are considered very low performers. Students who performed at or above the Intermediate international 
benchmark are considered to have attained the National Proficient Standard (NPS) for Australia, and 
demonstrated more than the minimal skills expected at a particular year level.
This section summarises the detailed findings in the report. Differences are only reported if tests of 
statistical significance showed that these were likely to be real differences; that is, differences were 
unlikely to be caused by chance.

Australia’s performance was higher than the TIMSS International average in both subjects, for both year

Executive summary
xix
Jurisdictions’ performance and proficiency over time
From TIMSS 2019 to 2023 
	
z Year 4 students in the Northern Territory and New South Wales improved in mathematics achievement 
by 36 points and 20 points, respectively. The improvement in the Northern Territory also included a 
significant increase in the proportion of students who achieved the NPS and a significant decrease in the 
percentage of very low performers. Tasmania’s performance declined by 22 points.
	
z Year 8 students in New South Wales declined in mathematics performance by 25 points.
	
z Year 4 students in the Northern Territory, New South Wales and Victoria improved in science achievement 
by 46, 22 and 18 points, respectively. Again, the improvement in the Northern Territory also included a 
significant increase in the proportion of students who achieved the NPS and a significant decrease in the 
percentage of very low performers.
	
z Year 8 students in New South Wales declined in science performance by 25 points.
From TIMSS 1995 to 2023 
	
z Year 4 students in New South Wales, Western Australia, Victoria, South Australia and Queensland 
improved in mathematics achievement by over 20 points. Most of these jurisdictions also experienced 
significant positive changes in the proportions who achieved the NPS and the percentages of very high 
and very low performers.
	
z Year 8 students in South Australia declined in mathematics performance by 18 points.
	
z Year 4 students in Queensland, New South Wales, Victoria and South Australia improved in science 
achievement by over 20 points. Most of these jurisdictions also experienced significant positive changes 
in the proportions who achieved the NPS and the percentages of very high and very low performers.
	
z Year 8 students in Victoria improved in science performance by 23 points.

Executive summary
Results for different demographic groups
Year 4 mathematics performance and proficiency
	
z Male students outperformed female students by 23 points, and also outperformed female students in the 
proportions who attained the NPS and the percentages of very high and very low performers.
	
z Students who had many books in the home scored 12 points higher than students who had an average 
number of books in the home, and 64 points higher than those who reported having a few books in the 
home.
	
z First Nations students scored an average of 462 points on the TIMSS Year 4 mathematics scale and 45% 
achieved the NPS.
	
z Students who spoke a language other than English at home had higher Year 4 mathematics achievement 
than those who spoke English at home (by 13 points). This was also reflected in the percentage of very 
high performers.
	
z 75% of students who attended schools in major cities achieved the NPS, compared to 64% who attended 
schools in regional areas.
Year 8 mathematics performance and proficiency
	
z Male students outperformed female students by 13 points, and also outperformed female students in 
terms of the proportions who achieved the NPS and the percentage of very high performers.
	
z Students who had many educational resources in the home scored 55 points higher than students who 
had some educational resources in the home, and 110 points higher than those who reported having few 
educational resources in the home.
	
z First Nations students scored an average of 435 points on the TIMSS Year 8 mathematics scale and 33% 
achieved the NPS.
	
z There was no significant difference in the average score in mathematics according to language 
background. However, the percentage of students who spoke a language other than English at home who 
were very high performers was significantly higher than students with an English-speaking background (by 
9 percentage points).
	
z 67% of students who attended schools in major cities achieved the NPS, compared to 55% who attended 
schools in regional areas.

z Male students outperformed female students by 10 points, and also outperformed female students in the

than English at home. The percentage of students who spoke English at home who were very low 
performers was significantly lower than students who spoke a language other than English at home (by 6 
percentage points).
	
z 72% of students who attended schools in major cities, compared to 64% who attended schools in regional

xxv
xxv
READER’S GUIDE
Sample surveys
TIMSS is conducted as a sample survey in most countries. In a sample survey, a sample of students is 
selected to represent the population of students at a particular year level in a given country. The samples 
are designed and conducted so that they provide reliable estimates about their representative population. 
A sample survey is more economical for a school to undertake and less of a burden than a full census of a 
population.
The sample design for TIMSS is known as ‘a two-stage stratified cluster sample design’. Stage 1 samples the 
schools and Stage 2 identifies a single mathematics classroom selected at random from the target year level 
in the chosen sampled schools.
Statistical weights are used to adjust for any differences arising from intended features of the design (for 
example, to over-sample minorities) or non-participation by students who were selected. In this way we can 
provide measures of achievement for the population, based on the responses of a sample. Please refer to 
the TIMSS 2023 technical report (Von Davier, 2023) for more information about sampling in TIMSS.
TIMSS achievement scales
TIMSS uses item response theory (IRT) methods to summarise the achievement of students on a scale with 
a mean of 500 and a standard deviation of 100 (please refer to the TIMSS 2023 technical report (Von Davier, 
2023) for more information about IRT methods). It should be noted that the results for Year 4 and Year 8 
should not be compared, nor should the results for mathematics and science at a particular year level. While 
the scales are expressed in the same numerical units, they are not directly comparable such that conclusions 
could be drawn about how much learning in mathematics equals how much learning in science (or how 
much learning at Year 4 equals how much learning at Year 8). That is, achievement on the TIMSS scales 
cannot be described in absolute terms (like all such scales developed using IRT technology). Comparisons 
can be made only in terms of relative performance (higher or lower), for example, among countries and 
population groups as well as over time.
The TIMSS mathematics and science scales for Year 4 and Year 8 were based on those established in 
the first cycle of the study (TIMSS 1995) and the methodology enables comparable trend measures from 
assessment to assessment within each year level (refer to the TIMSS 2023 technical report (Von Davier, 
2023) for more information).

xxvi
Reader’s guide
International comparison statistics
Several international comparison statistics are given in the report: the TIMSS international average and the 
international median.
The international average is the mean score or percentage of all countries who participated in TIMSS 2023 at 
that year level.
The international median is the midpoint in a ranking of countries that participated in TIMSS 2023 by score 
or percentage. By definition, half of the countries will have a score or percentage above the median and half 
below.
It should be noted that both the international average and the international median will be different 
depending on the set of countries included. Therefore, these statistics should be used in the context of a 
number of comparison statistics.
Standard errors and confidence intervals
In this and other reports, student achievement is often described by a mean or average score. For TIMSS, 
each mean score is calculated from the sample of students who undertook the assessments. These sample 
means approximate the actual mean score (known as the population mean) that would have been derived 
had all students in Australia participated in the TIMSS assessment.
If another sample of students was chosen on a different day, it is highly likely that the sample mean would be 
slightly different. Indeed, the sample mean is just one point along the range of student achievement scores, 
so more information is needed to gauge whether the sample mean is an underestimation or overestimation 
of the population mean.
In this report, means are presented with an associated standard error. The standard error is an estimate of 
the error in the estimate of the population mean from the sample (refer to the TIMSS 2023 Technical Report 
for more information about the estimation of standard errors in TIMSS). The size of the sample, as well as 
the variance or spread of the scores within the sample, can affect the size of the standard error. Smaller 
samples, or samples with a greater spread in scores, will have larger standard errors.
The calculation of confidence intervals can assist our assessment of a sample mean’s precision as an 
estimate of the population mean. Confidence intervals provide a range of scores within which we are 
‘confident’ that the population mean actually lies. The confidence interval is within plus or minus 1.96 
standard errors of the sample mean. A larger standard error results in a larger confidence interval, and a 
greater likelihood that the confidence intervals of 2 means will overlap and, therefore, reduce any difference 
to non-significance (see the next section on statistical significance).
Statistical significance
The term ‘significantly’ is used throughout the report to describe a difference that meets the requirements 
of statistical significance at the 0.05 level, indicating that the difference is real, and would be found in at 
least 95 analyses out of 100 if the comparison were to be repeated. It is not to be confused with the term 
‘substantial’, which is qualitative and based on judgement rather than statistical comparisons. A difference 
may appear substantial but not be statistically significant (due to factors that affect the size of the standard 
errors around the estimate, for example) while another difference may seem small but reach statistical 
significance because the estimate was more accurate.
It should be noted that, for the calculation of differences between the content and cognitive sub-domains 
and mathematics and science overall, a statistical significance level of 0.01 was used. This was to allow for 
the fact that the sub-domains form are part of the overall mathematics or science domain and contribute to 
any statistic for that overall domain.

Reader’s guide
xxvii
Rounding of figures
Due to rounding to eliminate decimals, some percentages in tables and figures may not exactly add to 
the totals. Totals, differences and averages are always calculated on the basis of exact numbers and are 
rounded only after calculation. When standard errors have been rounded to one decimal place and the value 
0.0 is shown, this does not imply that the standard error is zero, but that it is smaller than 0.05.
Due to differences in rounding practices there may be some differences in the outcome of significance tests 
as presented in this report and the results presented in the international report (von Davier et al., 2024).
Reading the distribution graphs
Confidence interval
Mean
5th
95th
25th
percentile
75th
percentile
Distribution graphs are presented alongside mean achievement in some figures. These distribution graphs 
are presented as horizontal bars with degrees of shading. The left end of the bar marks the 5th percentile – 
this is the score below which 5% of the students have scored. The lightest shading on the left-hand end of 
the bar covers the range between the 5th and the 25th percentiles. The next band, a slightly darker shade, 
covers the range between the 25th percentile and the lower limit of the confidence interval for the mean. The 
dark band in the middle of the distribution graph is the confidence interval for the mean – that is, the dark 
band indicates a range within which we can claim to be ‘confident’ that the mean will lie. On the right-hand 
side of the bar, the medium level of shading indicates the range between the upper limit of the confidence 
interval and the 75th percentile. The lightest shading on the right-hand end of the bar covers the range 
between the 75th and the 95th percentiles, while the right end of the bar marks the 95th percentile – this is 
the score below which 95% of the students have scored (with the remaining 5% scoring above this).
Notes about participating countries
Some countries have long official names. To facilitate the reading of the TIMSS reports, these countries are 
referred to by their shortened form in the text (for example, Hong Kong, Iran, Korea and Macao), rather than 
their official names (for example,  Hong Kong SAR; Iran, Islamic Republic of; Korea, Republic of; Macao SAR).
Some countries choose to administer TIMSS to grades other than the fourth or eighth years of formal 
schooling. Norway chose to assess the fifth and ninth grades to obtain better comparisons with Denmark, 
Finland and Sweden. South Africa assessed the fifth and ninth grades to better suit their curricula and to 
maintain trend measurement. Türkiye also chose to assess students in the fifth grade.
Whilst TIMSS 2023 has transitioned fully to an online assessment, 9 countries conducted TIMSS 2023 as a 
paper-based assessment, but which only included 2019 trend items (Bulgaria, Cote d’lvoire, Cyprus, Iran, Iraq, 
Kuwait, Morocco and South Africa).
New Zealand and Cote d’lvoire did not satisfy the guidelines for minimum school participation rates at the 
Year 8 level, and therefore achievement data could not be reliably estimated. New Zealand and Cote d’lvoire 
results have been reported “below the line” in the International Results in Mathematics and Science at Year 
8 and has not been included in calculating the TIMSS International average or reported in trends. Results for 
New Zealand and Cote d’lvoire for TIMSS 2023 have not been included in this report. 
Iraq administered the Year 4 TIMSS 2023 assessment but is not included in the TIMSS 2023 International 
Results (and therefore is not included in this report).

xxviii
Reader’s guide
Trends
This report contains information about trends in achievement over time. The following should be noted when 
reading about trends.
Internationally, there was no Year 4 assessment in 1999. Additionally, the Australian Year 8 sample that 
participated in 1999 was not comparable to that in other cycles, so trend results are not provided for 
Australia at Year 8 in 1999. In most figures and tables in this report, 1999 has been left out completely as 
there is no data for Australia. For visual accuracy, some figures (mainly line graphs) show 1999 as a year 
where no data was collected.
Trend data for the content and cognitive domains are available only from 2007, as earlier cycles did not 
include enough common items within each domain for trends to be established.
It should be noted that in 2015, the IEA (International Association for the Evaluation of Educational 
Achievement) made a change in the method of calculating standard errors, which means that standard 
errors for data from past cycles in this report will not match those presented in reports before 2015 (please 
refer to the TIMSS 2023 technical report (Von Davier et al., 2023) for more information on calculation of 
standard errors).
The National Proficient Standard
The Measurement framework for schooling in Australia 2020 (ACARA, 2020, updated 2023) has set the 
National Proficient Standard for TIMSS mathematics and science as the Intermediate international 
benchmark. This framework is the basis for reporting on progress towards the national education goals (the 
Alice Springs (Mparntwe) education declaration, Education Council, 2019). Proficient standards represent a 
‘challenging but reasonable’ expectation of student achievement.
Very low and very high performers
As well as the National Proficient Standard, this report includes the percentages of very low and very high 
performers as measures of student performance.
Very low performers are students who scored below the Low international benchmark (lower than 400 
points) and struggled to display even basic mathematical or science knowledge and understanding.
Very high performers are students who scored at the Advanced international benchmark (higher than 625 
points) and were highly proficient in mathematics or science at that year level.
Definitions of background characteristics
There are various definitions used in this report that are particular to the Australian context, as well as many 
that are used internationally. This section provides an explanation for those that are not self-evident.
Jurisdictions
Collectively, Australian states and territories are also referred to as jurisdictions.
Gender
For reporting purposes, the gender of the student is taken from information provided by the school. In TIMSS 
2023, in Australia, the options were female, male or ‘other’. In TIMSS 2023, less than 1% of Australian Year 
4 and Year 8 students were identified as ‘other’, meaning that reporting of results for these students is not 
possible.

Reader’s guide
xxix
Number of books in the home
This variable is used as a proxy for socioeconomic status, where information about parents’ occupations, 
education and wealth are not available. It is derived from student self-reports of the number of books in their 
homes. Their responses have been grouped so that:
	
z a few books equals 25 or fewer books
	
z an average number of books equals between 26 and 200 books
	
z many books equals more than 200 books. 
While the relationship between the number of books in the home and student achievement is not definitive, 
there is a very strong relationship between the two.
Parental education
Parental education is a component of socioeconomic status. Year 8 students were asked to indicate the 
highest level of education attained by each of their parents or guardians. For the analyses in this report, the 
responses from both questions were combined to identify the highest level of education attained by either 
parent. Where no response is given for one parent, the response for the other parent was used. Where no 
information was given for either parent, parental education was recorded as missing.
Please note that, as Australia did not participate in the home questionnaire, completed by parents, 
information about parental education is not available for Year 4 students.
Educational resources in the home
The presence or absence of educational resources in the home expresses potential advantage or 
disadvantage for students that may reflect the ability of parents to provide materially for their children or 
indicate differences in practical and psychological support for academic achievement. These resources may 
be physical, such as books or an internet connection, or take the form of more intangible attributes such as 
parental education or occupation.
The Home educational resources scale was created, using Year 8 students’ responses to 3 items:
	
z parents’ educational background
	
z number of books in the home
	
z home study supports – students having their own room and an internet connection at home.
Students with many resources had a score on the scale of at least 11.4, which corresponded to their 
reporting that they had more than 25 books in the home along with both home study supports (own room 
and an internet connection), and that at least one of their parents had finished university, on average. In 
contrast, students with few resources had a scale score no higher than 9.1, which corresponded to their 
reporting that they had 10 or fewer books in the home, that they had both home study supports, and that 
neither of their parents had proceeded beyond upper secondary school, on average. All other students were 
classified as having some resources.
The cut-points for the Home education resources index have been adjusted for TIMSS 2023. In TIMSS 
2019, students with many resources had a score on the scale that corresponded to their reporting that they 
had more than 100 books in the home along with both home study supports (own room and an internet 
connection), and that at least one of their parents had finished university, on average. Students with few 
resources had a scale score that corresponded to their reporting that they had 25 or fewer books in the 
home, that they had neither their own room nor an internet connection, and that neither of their parents had 
proceeded beyond upper secondary school, on average. Therefore, the index has shifted along the scale 
since TIMSS 2019.
Please note that, as Australia did not participate in an early learning survey, completed by parents, 
information about parental education, and therefore the Home Resources for Learning scale, is not available 
for Year 4 students.

xxx
Reader’s guide
First Nations background
First Nations background data were derived from the student questionnaire, which asked students whether 
they identified as being of Aboriginal and/or Torres Strait Islander descent. For the purpose of this report, 
data for the 2 groups are presented together under the terms ‘First Nations students’ and ‘Non-First Nations 
students’.
Language spoken at home
The language spoken at home variable is derived from student self-report of how often English was spoken 
at home. Where the student spoke English ‘never’ or only ‘sometimes’, the student was considered to speak 
a language other than English at home. Those who indicated that they spoke English ‘always’ or ‘almost 
always’ were considered English speakers in the home environment.
Geographic location of the school
In Australia, in TIMSS 2023, the participating schools were coded with using the Australian Bureau of 
Statistics’ Australian statistical geography standard (ASGS) remoteness structure, (ABS, 2011), which has 
superseded the MCEETYA classification of geographic location.
	
z The following categories are used to report geographic location using the ASGS remoteness structure: 
major cities, which includes all major cities of Australia
	
z regional areas, which includes all inner regional and outer regional areas in Australia
	
z remote areas, which includes all remote and very remote areas in Australia.

1
CHAPTER 1
INTRODUCTION
1.1	
What is TIMSS?
The Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) is an international study that 
assesses the mathematical and scientific knowledge and understanding of Year 4 and Year 8 students. 
TIMSS is directed by the International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA), an 
independent international cooperative of national research institutions and government agencies that has 
been conducting studies of cross-national achievement in a wide range of subjects since 1959.
Australia has participated in TIMSS since the first cycle in 1995 and in every 4-year cycle since then and over 
this 28-year period has collected rich data about trends in mathematics and science achievement. The 2023 
cycle of TIMSS completed its transition to a completely online assessment.
To inform educational policy in the participating countries, TIMSS also collects extensive background 
information that addresses concerns about the quantity, quality and content of instruction. This background 
information is collected through a series of questionnaires for students, parents, teachers, principals and 
curriculum specialists.
Australia was one of 64 countries, and 6 regions or benchmarking participants,1 that participated in TIMSS 
2023 together with around 5,500 Year 4 students and 8,500 Year 8 students. These students completed the 
tests in mathematics and science and answered questionnaires on their background and experiences in 
learning these subjects at school. School principals and the students’ mathematics and science teachers 
also completed detailed questionnaires.
For comparability across countries and across assessments, data collection is conducted at the end of 
the school year – typically, March to June in northern-hemisphere countries and August to November in 
southern-hemisphere countries.
TIMSS 2023 was managed by the TIMSS & PIRLS International Study Center at Boston College in the United 
States. Sampling procedures were overseen by RTI International and the Sampling Unit at IEA Hamburg. The 
IEA Secretariat and the TIMSS & PIRLS International Study Center oversaw the translation and verification 
processes as well as the quality-assurance program; and IEA Hamburg was responsible for overseeing the 
data collection, data processing and data analysis.
Appendix A provides more information about the operations and procedures involved in TIMSS 2023.
1	
A benchmarking participant is a province or region that participated in TIMSS for its own internal benchmarking. Data from these provinces are not included in the 
international averages or medians and are not included in this national report.

2
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
1.1.1	
What are the main goals of TIMSS?
The main goal of TIMSS is to assist countries to monitor and evaluate their mathematics and science 
teaching across time and across year levels. TIMSS offers countries an opportunity to:
	
z collect comprehensive and internationally comparable data about the mathematics and science concepts, 
processes and attitudes that students have learnt by Year 4 and Year 8
	
z assess progress internationally in mathematics and science learning across time for students in Year 4 
and Year 8
	
z understand the contexts in which students learn best since TIMSS enables international comparisons of 
the key policy variables in relation to school curricula, modes of instruction and provision of resources 
that result in higher levels of student achievement
	
z use TIMSS to address internal policy issues – for example, to examine the performance of population 
subgroups and address equity concerns.
1.1.2	
Research model for IEA studies
TIMSS is based on a research model that uses the curriculum, in broad terms, as its foundation. The TIMSS 
curriculum model includes 3 curriculum levels, considered in relation to the context in which they occur. 
These levels are shown in Figure 1.1.
National, social
and educational
context
School, teacher
and classroom
context
Student
outcomes and
characteristics
Intended
curriculum
Implemented
curriculum
Attained
curriculum
FIGURE 1.1 The TIMSS curriculum model
The research questions associated with each of the curriculum levels are:
	
z the intended curriculum – defined as the curriculum as specified at national or system level. What are 
mathematics and science students around the world expected to learn? How do countries vary in their 
intended goals, and what characteristics of education systems, schools and students influence the 
development of these goals? How should the education system be organised to facilitate this learning?
	
z the implemented curriculum – defined as the curriculum as interpreted and delivered by classroom 
teachers. What is actually taught in classrooms? Who teaches it? What opportunities are provided for 
students to learn mathematics and science? How do instructional practices vary among countries and 
what factors influence these variations?
	
z the attained curriculum – which is the part of the curriculum that is learnt by students, as demonstrated 
by their attitudes and achievements. What mathematics and science concepts, processes and attitudes 
have students learnt? What factors are linked to students’ opportunity to learn, and how do these factors 
influence students’ achievements?

Chapter 1 Introduction
3
The data describing the intended curriculum were gathered through the curriculum questionnaire, which 
asked about the mathematics and science curricula, school organisational approaches and instructional 
practices. This extensive questionnaire was developed in Australia by the Australian Council for Educational 
Research (ACER), reviewed by curriculum experts in each state and territory education department, and then 
submitted to the International Study Center. Further information about the curriculum and education policies 
is available through the TIMSS 2023 encyclopedia (Reynolds et al., 2024).
1.1.3	
The TIMSS assessment framework
The TIMSS assessment framework provides the conceptual underpinning of the TIMSS 2023 assessment 
instruments. As TIMSS assesses both mathematics and science, the 2 subjects are treated separately within 
the assessment framework. Each subject is organised around 2 dimensions: a content dimension and a 
cognitive dimension. The content dimension specifies the subject matter to be assessed within mathematics 
or science, while the cognitive dimension specifies the thinking processes to be assessed.
The content domains differ for Year 4 and Year 8 students, reflecting the nature and difficulty of the 
mathematics and science widely taught at each year level. For example, in science at Year 8, Physics and 
Chemistry are assessed as separate content domains, and receive more emphasis than at Year 4, where they 
are assessed as one content domain, Physical science. In contrast, the cognitive domains are the same for 
Year 4 and Year 8 (nuanced to accommodate the particular requirements of each year level), and encompass 
a range of cognitive processes involved in working mathematically or scientifically and in problem-solving. 
These processes hold their currency throughout the primary and middle school years.
Further details about the content and cognitive domains covered in each subject at Year 4 and Year 8 are 
provided in Chapter 2 and Chapter 5.
1.2	
Changes in TIMSS 2023
1.2.1	
Computer-based assessment
The 2023 cycle of TIMSS completed its transition to a digital assessment; this was the first time it was 
offered as a completely online assessment. TIMSS 2023 made innovations to the assessment through the 
benefits of the digital platform, which included:
	
z presenting complex and innovative item types that captured both responses and process indicators
	
z interactive opportunities and provision of digital tools for students to use in developing and providing their 
responses
	
z problem-solving and inquiry (PSI) tasks based on interactive scenarios that allowed students to follow a 
series of steps towards a solution or goal
	
z the increased use of automated scoring to improve scoring accuracy and comparability across countries
	
z research into using machine learning to evaluate graphical and short written responses.
In addition to the innovations associated with the digital assessment, TIMSS 2023 introduced a new group 
adaptive assessment design. This aimed to better match the difficulty of TIMSS assessment items to a 
country’s students’ level of achievement (based on mean achievement in the previous cycle of TIMSS).
Key features of the TIMSS 2023 group adaptive assessment design are:
	
z items were grouped into blocks based on 3 difficulty levels (easy, medium or difficult)
	
z for each subject, a student was assigned a block of easy and a block of medium items, or a block of 
medium and a block of difficult items

4
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
	
z all countries took all items, but the proportion of less and more difficult items was adjusted in some 
countries. In countries where it was anticipated students might struggle to answer many difficult items, 
less difficult booklets were administered at higher rates. In known high-performing countries more difficult 
booklets were administered.
Australia participated in TIMSS 2023 with the standard rotation of equal proportions of more and less 
difficult booklets.
1.2.2	
Environmental awareness
TIMSS 2023 expanded its scope to assess environmental knowledge and attitudes. This component 
provides valuable insights into students’ understanding of environmental issues, including climate change, 
biodiversity, and conservation. As well as information about students’ environmental knowledge, TIMSS 
2023 collected data on student attitudes toward the natural environment and engagement in environmentally 
responsible behaviours. By assessing both knowledge and attitudes, TIMSS 2023 provided a more 
comprehensive understanding of students’ environmental literacy, which is essential for empowering them 
to make informed decisions about sustainability and becoming active participants in addressing global 
environmental challenges. TIMSS 2023 also included questions for teachers and schools in the context 
questionnaires, for example, asking about schools’ emphasis on environmental sustainability; the methods 
science teachers use to teach sustainability; as well as the extent to which science teachers encourage 
environmentally responsible behaviour among students.
1.3	
Components of TIMSS 2023
In Australia, students who participated in TIMSS 2023 completed an online assessment that contained 
an even distribution of mathematics and science items. The online assessment was designed to be 
administered in 2 sessions, separated by a short break. Year 4 sessions were 36 minutes and Year 8 
sessions were 45 minutes. Each student also completed a student questionnaire.
1.3.1	
The assessment task
As TIMSS focuses on international curricula in mathematics and science, many test items were required to 
cover the range of topics and abilities. Given that no individual student could be expected to complete the 
total number of items in a reasonable length of time, mathematics and science items were grouped into 
clusters. These clusters were rotated through 14 item blocks (at each year level), such that each cluster 
was addressed in more than one booklet (administered online). Each booklet contained 4 clusters – 2 for 
mathematics and 2 for science – and comprised item types, including multiple-choice and constructed-
response items.
Each participating student completed only one of the 14 booklets, which were evenly distributed within 
classes. Accordingly, only 2 or 3 students in each class completed a particular booklet. Further information 
on the TIMSS assessment booklets and the types of items students attempted to complete is presented in 
Appendix A.
1.3.2	
The contextual questionnaires
Central to the TIMSS research model is the idea that it is important to understand the contexts in which 
students learn, as well as to assess achievement. After the achievement data were collected from students, 
each student completed a student questionnaire. Teacher and school questionnaires were also administered 
to the mathematics and science teacher(s) of each selected class and to the principal of the school.

Chapter 1 Introduction
5
The student questionnaire sought information on home contexts, and on students’ characteristics and 
attitudes towards learning mathematics and science. It also sought information on their attitudes toward the 
natural environment and engagement in environmentally responsible behaviours.
The teacher questionnaire asked about teacher preparation and experience, pedagogical practices, use of 
technology, assessment, assignment of homework, school and classroom climate, and whether the TIMSS 
topics had been covered in class.
The school questionnaire, answered by the principal (or the principal’s delegate), sought descriptive 
information about school characteristics, instructional time, resources and technology, school climate for 
learning, students’ school readiness, and principal preparation and experience.
1.4	
TIMSS 2023 participants
1.4.1	
Internationally
A total of 58 countries at Year 4 (and 6 benchmarking entities) and 44 countries at Year 8 (and 3 
benchmarking entities) participated in TIMSS 2023. In total, over 650,000 students participated worldwide. 
Eight countries completed a paper-based assessment that comprised TIMSS 2019 trend items only. The 
participating countries are shown in Table 1.1.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
1.4.2	
Within Australia
The international sample design for TIMSS is generally referred to as ‘a two-stage stratified cluster sample 
design’. Stage 1 samples schools, which in Australia are stratified by jurisdiction,2 sector, geographic location 
and a socioeconomic variable. The intention is that the sample drawn will be representative of each of these 
strata. Stage 2 samples one or two intact classrooms from the target year in sampled schools.
In most countries, 150 schools and one classroom in each school were selected to participate (resulting 
in at least 4,000 participating students per country). In some countries, including Australia, a larger sample 
of schools and students participated to allow for meaningful comparisons to be made between different 
sections of the school population. This enabled the production of reliable estimates for each of the 
Australian jurisdictions.
In Australia, 283 primary schools and 276 secondary schools participated in the TIMSS 2023 data collection. 
At least one intact class from the relevant year level from each school was selected, resulting in a sample of 
5,470 Year 4 students and 8,442 Year 8 students. For more information about sampling and the Australian 
TIMSS sample, please refer to Appendix A.
Statistical weighting enables the sampled students to represent the total student population at each year 
level (for more information about weighting, please refer to the Reader’s guide). The weighted numbers for 
Australia for Year 4 and Year 8, along with the numbers of participating schools and students, are shown in 
Table 1.2.3

1.5	
TIMSS and Australia’s National Assessment Program
TIMSS is a key part of the National Assessment Program (NAP). Components of NAP include the National 
Assessment Program – Literacy and Numeracy (NAPLAN), which is conducted annually for every 
student in Years 3, 5, 7 and 9; the national sample assessments of civics and citizenship, information and 
communication technology (ICT) literacy, and science literacy; and the international assessments, which 
comprise – in addition to TIMSS – the IEA’s Progress in International Reading Literacy Study (PIRLS) and 
the Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development’s Programme for International Student 
Assessment (PISA).
Results from these NAP assessments allow for nationally comparable reporting of progress towards the 
Alice Springs (Mparntwe) education declaration (Education Council, 2019). These declarations set out a vision 
for education in Australia that promotes excellence and equity to enable all Australians to become confident 
and creative individuals, successful learners, and active and informed community members.
The Australian Curriculum, Assessment and Reporting Authority (ACARA) reports on the NAP assessments 
annually in its National report on schooling in Australia, which is the main vehicle for reporting against 
nationally agreed key performance measures defined in the Measurement framework for schooling in 
Australia 2020 (ACARA, 2023). This Framework outlines national standards for each of the element of 
the NAP, including TIMSS. The national standard for TIMSS is a proficient standard, which represents a 
‘challenging but reasonable’ expectation of student achievement with students needing to demonstrate 
more than elementary skills expected at a particular year level. The National Proficient Standard for TIMSS 
mathematics and science is the TIMSS Intermediate international benchmark (please refer to Chapter 2 and 
Chapter 5 for more information about the TIMSS Intermediate international benchmarks for mathematics 
and science at Year 4 and Year 8).

8
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
1.6	
Structure of this report
The results of TIMSS 2023 and Australia’s performance are presented in 2 reports: Volume I focuses 
specifically on the achievement results, detailing Australia’s results within the international context, as well 
as a presentation of the results for the Australian jurisdictions and for different demographic groups within 
Australia, including male and female students. This report also examines TIMSS 2023 results in the content 
and cognitive domains and environmental awareness. Volume II, to be published in 2025, will present the 
results from the contextual questionnaires, examining the contexts in which learning and achievement occur, 
including home, school, and classroom contexts, as well as student attitudes.
Volume I is structured in 2 parts: Part A focuses on the mathematics results and Part B on the science 
results, including environmental awareness. Each part contains 3 chapters, the first of which describes the 
assessment framework for the relevant subject followed by 2 chapters that contain the results for Year 4 and 
Year 8, respectively.

PART A
MATHEMATICS

10
10
KEY FINDINGS
YEAR 4 MATHEMATICS
	
z Australian students scored an average of 525 points on the TIMSS Year 4 mathematics scale.
	
z Australia outperformed 33 countries, including the United States, Canada and New Zealand, but 
was outperformed by 14 countries, including England and Ireland, as well as Singapore, the  
top-performing country.
	
z 72% of Australian students achieved the National Proficient Standard (NPS). Most students 
(over 90%) in the highest performing countries of Singapore, Chinese Taipei, Korea, Hong Kong 
and Japan reached this level.
	
z 13% of Australian Year 4 students were very high performers and 9% were very low performers. 
In comparison, 49% of students in Singapore were very high performers and only 2% were very 
low performers.
	
z Australia’s Year 4 mathematics achievement in TIMSS 2023 was 30 points higher than in 1995 
and 9 points higher than in 2019.
	
z Since 1995, the proportion of students who achieved the NPS has increased from 61% to 72%.
	
z Year 4 students in the Northern Territory and New South Wales improved in mathematics 
achievement from 2019 by 36 points and 20 points, respectively. For the Northern Territory, this 
was accompanied by a significant increase in the proportion of students who achieved the NPS 
and a significant decrease in the percentage of very low performers.
	
z Year 4 students in New South Wales, Western Australia, Victoria, South Australia and 
Queensland improved in mathematics achievement since 1995 by over 20 points. Most of these 
jurisdictions also experienced significant positive changes in the proportions who achieved the 
NPS and the percentages of very high and very low performers.
	
z Male students outperformed female students by 23 points, and also outperformed female 
students in terms of the proportion who achieved the NPS and the percentages of very high and 
very low performers.
	
z Students who had many books in the home scored 12 points higher than students who had an 
average number of books in the home, and 64 points higher than those who reported having a 
few books in the home.
	
z First Nations students scored an average of 462 points on the TIMSS Year 4 mathematics scale 
and 45% achieved the NPS.
	
z Students who spoke a language other than English at home had higher Year 4 mathematics 
achievement than those who spoke English at home (by 13 points). This was also reflected in 
the percentage of very high performers.
	
z 75% of students who attended schools in major cities, compared to 64% who attended schools 
in regional areas, achieved the NPS.
	
z Australian Year 4 students performed relatively higher in the content domain of Data, but 
relatively lower in Number. The score for Measurement and geometry was not significantly 
different to the overall mathematics score.
	
z Australian Year 4 students performed relatively higher in the cognitive domain of Knowing, but 
relatively lower in Applying. The score for Reasoning was not significantly different to the overall 
mathematics score.

11
11
KEY FINDINGS
YEAR 8 MATHEMATICS
	
z Australian students scored an average of 509 points on the TIMSS Year 8 mathematics scale.
	
z Australia outperformed 25 countries, including the United States and the United Arab Emirates, 
but was outperformed by 8 countries, including England and Ireland, as well as Singapore, the 
top-performing country.
	
z 64% of Australian students achieved the National Proficient Standard (NPS) in TIMSS Year 
8 mathematics. Most students (over 85%) in the highest performing countries of Singapore, 
Chinese Taipei, Korea, Japan and Hong Kong reached this level.
	
z 11% of Australian Year 8 students were very high performers and 13% were very low performers. 
In comparison, 46% of students in Singapore were very high performers and only 3% were very 
low performers.
	
z Australia’s Year 8 mean score in mathematics achievement has not changed significantly since 
either 2019 or 1995.
	
z There has been no change in the proportion of students who achieved the NPS since either 2019 
or 1995.
	
z There was very little significant change in performance over time, across the jurisdictions. South 
Australia declined in performance (by 18 points) since 1995, while New South Wales declined in 
performance (by 25 points) since 2019.
	
z Male students outperformed female students by 13 points, and also outperformed female 
students in terms of the proportion who achieved the NPS and the percentages of very high 
performers.
	
z Students who had many educational resources in the home scored 55 points higher than 
students who had some educational resources in the home, and 110 points higher than those 
who reported having few educational resources in the home.
	
z First Nations students scored an average of 435 points on the TIMSS Year 8 mathematics scale 
and 33% achieved the NPS.
	
z There was no significant difference in the average score in mathematics according to language 
background. However, the percentage of students who spoke a language other than English 
at home and were very high performers was significantly higher than that of students with an 
English-speaking background (by 9 percentage points).
	
z 67% of students who attended schools in major cities, compared to 55% who attended schools 
in regional areas, achieved the NPS.
	
z Australian Year 8 students performed relatively higher in the content domain of Data and 
probability, but relatively lower in Number and Algebra. The score for Geometry and measurement 
was not significantly different to the overall mathematics score.
	
z Australian Year 8 students performed relatively lower in the cognitive domain of Reasoning. 
The scores for Knowing and Applying were not significantly different to the overall mathematics 
score.

CHAPTER 2
THE TIMSS 2023 MATHEMATICS 
FRAMEWORK AND BENCHMARKS
Many education specialists know that when children understand mathematics, they have a better chance of 
participating equitably in society. Philpot et al. (2021) detailed this further with:
All children can benefit from developing an understanding of mathematics and facility with the mathematics 
needed in today’s technological world. Mathematics is essential in daily life as well as in many career 
fields such as engineering, architecture, accounting, banking, business, medicine, ecology, and aerospace. 
Mathematics is vital to economics and finance, as well as to computing technology and software 
development. The ability to learn new skills and to solve problems is paramount in today’s changing world. 
(p.5)
The TIMSS 2023 mathematics framework (Philpot et al., 2021) is organised around 2 dimensions – a content 
dimension, which specifies the subject matter to be assessed within mathematics (for example, Number, 
Algebra etc.) and a cognitive dimension, which specifies the thinking processes and sets of behaviours 
expected of students as they engage with the mathematics content.
2.1	
Year 4 and Year 8 mathematics content and cognitive domains
The TIMSS 2023 mathematics framework (2021) defines 3 content domains for Year 4 students (Number; 
Measurement and geometry; Data) and 4 content domains for Year 8 students (Number; Algebra; Geometry 
and measurement; Data and probability). Each of these content domains has several topic areas. For 
example, in Year 4, Number includes whole numbers; expressions, simple equations and relationships; and 
fractions and decimals, but in Year 8, Number includes integers; fractions and decimals; and proportions, 
ratios and percentages. These topic areas are shown in Table 2.1.
For a detailed description of each of the content domains in mathematics, please refer to the TIMSS 2023 
mathematics framework (2021).

Chapter 2 The TIMSS 2023 mathematics framework and benchmarks
13
To respond correctly to TIMSS test items, students need to be familiar with the mathematics content of the 
items. Just as importantly, however, items are designed to elicit the use of particular cognitive skills. The 
TIMSS 2023 assessment framework (Mullis, et al., 2021) presents detailed descriptions of the skills and 
abilities that make up the cognitive domains and that are assessed in conjunction with the content. The 
student behaviours encompassed by the cognitive dimension have been classified into 3 domains within the 
assessment framework. These are described as follows:
	
z Knowing – the facts, concepts and procedures students need to know
	
z Applying – the ability of students to apply knowledge and conceptual understanding in a range of 
situations
	
z Reasoning – involves the logical, systematic thinking that students need to use to generate and justify 
solutions to problems, make inferences, and deal with complex relationships between mathematical 
objects.
Table 2.2 shows the percentage of time devoted to each cognitive domain at Year 4 and Year 8. These 
3 cognitive domains are used for both Year 4 and Year 8, but the testing time differs in each year level, 
reflecting the difference in age and experience of the students tested.
In TIMSS 2023, each content domain included items designed to address each of the 3 cognitive domains; 
for example, the Number domain included Knowing, Applying and Reasoning items, as did the other content 
domains.

2.2	
Year 4 and Year 8 mathematics international benchmarks
The TIMSS mathematics achievement scales summarise Year 4 and Year 8 student performance when 
interacting with a variety of mathematical tasks and questions (see the Reader’s guide for more information 
about the achievement scales). Student achievement is based on the responses to test questions designed 
to assess a range of content areas. When comparing groups of students across and within countries, 
summary statistics such as the average, or mean, scale score are often used. This score, however, does not 
provide detailed information as to what types of mathematical tasks the students were able to undertake 
successfully. Instead, TIMSS uses international benchmarks to provide descriptions of achievement on the 
scales in relation to performance on the questions asked.
Internationally, it was decided that performance should be measured at 4 levels. These levels summarise the 
achievement reached by:
	
z the Advanced international benchmark, which was set at 625 points
	
z the High international benchmark, which was set at 550 points
	
z the Intermediate international benchmark, which was set at 475 points
	
z the Low international benchmark, which was set at 400 points.
The descriptions of the levels are cumulative, so that a student who reached the High benchmark typically 
demonstrated the knowledge and skills for both the Intermediate and the Low benchmarks. Table 2.3 
provides a summary of the TIMSS 2023 Year 4 and Year 8 mathematics benchmarks.

and solve problems involving outcomes and probabilities in 
familiar contexts.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
2.2.1	
The National Proficient Standard
In Australia, the Intermediate international benchmark has been designated as the National Proficient 
Standard. This level represents ‘a challenging but reasonable’ level of performance where students need to 
demonstrate more than the minimal skills expected (ACARA, 2023).
At Year 4, students at the Intermediate benchmark can demonstrate mathematical knowledge in simple 
situations and relate representations. This means that students can perform computations with 3-digit 
whole numbers in a variety of situations and they can add and order simple decimals. They can also 
measure straight distances, describe 3-dimensional shapes and use data from multiple sources to relate 
representations.
For example, Figure 2.1 shows an item from Data, demonstrating the cognitive skill of Applying. Students 
were asked to work out missing values in a table.
At Year 8, students at the Intermediate benchmark can apply mathematical knowledge in a variety of 
situations. This means that students can solve problems across contexts involving whole numbers, negative 
numbers, fractions, decimals, and proportional relationships. They can interpret relationships given visually 
or in words to represent them algebraically. Students at this level demonstrate some understanding of angle 
measures and in relating 2-dimensional and 3-dimensional shapes. They can read, interpret, and integrate 
across sources to represent data.
In the example shown in Figure 2.2, from the content domain Algebra, students were asked to solve a worded 
problem involving an inequality and explain their answer. This item demonstrates the cognitive skill of 
Reasoning.
Further information about the types of mathematics skills and strategies demonstrated by Year 4 and Year 
8 students who performed at each of the international benchmarks, along with examples of the types of 
responses given by students at each of the benchmarks, is provided in the TIMSS 2023 international results in 
mathematics and science (von Davier et al., 2024).

CHAPTER 3
AUSTRALIA’S YEAR 4  
MATHEMATICS RESULTS
In this report, the focus is on differences that are statistically significant (in other words, are unlikely 
to have occurred by chance). Where the commentary states that there was a difference between sets 
of numbers, whether these were scores, percentages or percentage point differences, it means that 
the difference satisfied this condition. Where the commentary states that there was no difference, or 
where no comment is made regarding a possible comparison, it indicates that the difference was not 
statistically significant.
Due to large standard errors for some results, differences that appear numerically larger may not be 
statistically significant. For more information about standard errors and statistical significance, please 
refer to the Reader’s Guide.
3.1	
Year 4 mathematics results within the international context
This section reports the TIMSS 2023 mathematics results as average scores and distributions on the TIMSS 
Year 4 mathematics scale (please see the Reader’s guide for more information about the achievement 
scales).
Australian Year 4 students achieved a mean score of 525 points in mathematics. This performance was 
lower than students in 14 countries (including Singapore, Chinese Taipei, Korea, Hong Kong and Japan, 
England, Ireland, and the Netherlands); not significantly different to students in 10 countries (including 
Norway, Czech Republic, Finland, Germany, Belgium (Flemish) and Hungary) and higher than students in 33 
countries (including the United States, Canada, Spain, New Zealand and France).
Figure 3.1 provides the mean scores achieved by students in each country, the standard error confidence 
interval of each mean, and the standard deviation (an indication in the variability of individual scores 
around the mean). Countries are positioned from highest to lowest level of achievement; however, this is 
not a simple ranking as the differences between countries may not be statistically significant. The shading 
indicates whether the score for a given country was significantly different to Australia. To determine whether 
or not differences between other countries were statistically significant, please refer to the TIMSS 2023 
international results in mathematics and science (von Davier et al., 2024).

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
Singapore was the top-performing country of TIMSS 2023, scoring 615 points. This was significantly higher 
than the scores for all other countries. Chinese Taipei (607 points) was the next highest, followed by Hong 
Kong and Korea (both 594 points), closely followed by Japan (591 points) and then Macao (582 points).
There was a large gap of over 20 points between Macao and the next highest performing countries, 
Lithuania, Türkiye and England. The mean score for these countries exceeded the High benchmark of 550 
points.
3.1.1	
Performance at the international benchmarks
In addition to analysing performance according to mean scores, the international benchmarks give further 
insight into student achievement.
Figure 3.2 shows the percentages of students in each country at each of the international benchmarks. The 
countries are ordered by the percentages of students who reached the Intermediate international benchmark, 
which is the National Proficient Standard (NPS) set for TIMSS mathematics in Australia (please see the 
Reader’s guide for more information about the NPS).
Students who attained the National Proficient Standard
In TIMSS 2023, 72% of Australian students attained the NPS in Year 4 mathematics, similar to the 
international median of 70%.
In Chinese Taipei, 97% of students achieved at least the Intermediate international benchmark. There were 
similar levels of achievement in Japan (95%), Singapore (93%) and Korea (93%).
Very high performers
In Australia, 13% of Year 4 students were very high performers in mathematics, compared to the international 
median of 7%.
The countries with the highest average mathematics achievement were also the countries with the largest 
percentages of students who reached the Advanced benchmark. In Singapore, 49% of Year 4 students were 
very high performers, 40 % in Chinese Taipei, 38% in Hong Kong, 36% in Korea, and 32% in Japan. Macao 
followed with 30% of very high performers, Türkiye with 24% and England with 22%.
Australia was one of 21 countries that had 10% or more of very high performing students. Nine countries 
(Morocco, Kosovo, Uzbekistan, Saudi Arabia, Kuwait, Iran, Chile, Brazil, and Bosnia and Herzegovina) had 1% 
of students who attained the Advanced benchmark.
Very low performers
In Australia, 9% of Year 4 students were very low performers in mathematics, similar to the international 
median of 9%.
In Chinese Taipei, 100% of students achieved at least the Low international benchmark, meaning that there 
were no very low performers.
In Korea and Japan, only 1% of students were very low performers; Singapore, Hong Kong, Macao and 
Netherlands had 2% of students.
While the proportion of very low performers was equal to or less than 10% in many countries, the proportion 
was 20% or more in 16 countries and more than 50% in in 4 countries (Brazil, Morocco, Kuwait and South 
Africa).

3.1.2	
Trends in Year 4 mathematics achievement
Looking at the overall trends in Year 4 mathematics achievement between 2019 and 2023, just under half 
of the participating countries had no significant change in score. Almost equal numbers of countries had 
increases and decreases in average mathematics achievement. However, for countries that participated in 
the 1995 and 2023 cycles, most had increases and very few had either a decrease or no change in average 
mathematics achievement.
Since 2019:
	
z 14 out of 49 countries had increases in average mathematics achievement
	
z 13 out of 49 countries had decreases in average mathematics achievement
	
z Poland had the highest increase in achievement (by 26 points), followed by Montenegro (by 24 points) 
then Saudi Arabia (by 21 points); Kazakhstan had the largest decrease (by 25 points)
	
z Australia’s average mathematics achievement increased by 9 points.
Since 1995:
	
z 12 out of 16 countries had increases in average mathematics achievement
	
z 2 out of 16 countries had decreases in average mathematics achievement
	
z Portugal had the highest increase in achievement (by 75 points), followed by England (by 68 points), 
Cyprus (by 41 points), Hong Kong (by 37 points), and Iran (by 33 points)
	
z Australia’s average mathematics achievement score increased by 30 points.
Patterns of achievement
Figure 3.3 presents trends in Year 4 mathematics achievement for selected countries with comparable data 
from TIMSS 1995 to 2023. The comparison countries are the United States, England, New Zealand, Singapore 
and Hungary, which have participated in all cycles since 1995. The patterns of achievement through 8 cycles 
of TIMSS have varied considerably across countries.4
4	
There was no Year 4 data collection in 1999, so only 7 cycles of data are available for Year 4.

FIGURE 3.3 Trends in Year 4 mathematics achievement scores, 1995–2023, selected countries  
Between 1995 and 2023, the following key trends are noted:
	
z England’s mean score, which had a sharp increase of 47 points between the 1995 and 2003 cycles, has 
continued to increase steadily over subsequent cycles to a peak in 2019 with a mean score of 556 points. 
This was not significantly different to the 2023 mean score of 552 points.
	
z Singapore’s mean score has steadily increased over time until a peak of 625 points in 2019; however, it 
significantly decreased in the 2023 cycle by 10 points to 615 points.
	
z The United States’ mean score increased by 11 points in the 2007 cycle and then a further 12 points in the 
2011 cycle. Between 2011 and 2023, there was a significant decrease of 25 points, from 541 points to 516 
points.

24
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
	
z New Zealand’s mean score, which had an increase of 24 points between the 1995 and 2003 cycles, has 
stayed steady since 2003 at around 490 points.
	
z Hungary’s mean score has fluctuated around the 520-point mark, with a high of 529 points in 2003 and 
2015 and a low of 510 points in 2007.
Australia’s average score in Year 4 mathematics increased significantly during the 28 years of data 
collection, with significant increases of 17 points between 2003 and 2007 and of 9 points between 2019 and 
2023.
Figure 3.4 shows the distribution of mathematics achievement for Australian Year 4 students across the 
cycles. The distribution of scores was the narrowest in 2003 and the largest in 2023.

FIGURE 3.4 Trends in Year 4 mathematics achievement score and distribution, 1995–2023, Australia 
Relative achievement
Table 3.1 displays each country’s position relative to Australia’s in each TIMSS cycle (please see Appendix B 
for the mean scores and standard errors by cycle for each country).
Australia’s relative position in TIMSS 2023 has improved since 2019. Norway (Year 5), the Czech Republic, 
Finland, Denmark, Belgium (Flemish) and Hungary all outperformed Australia in 2019 but were not 
significantly different to Australia in 2023. Portugal, the United States and Cyprus all outperformed Australia 
in 2019 but scored significantly lower than Australia in 2023. The scores for Slovak Republic, Italy, Canada, 
Azerbaijan and Kazakhstan were similar to Australia in 2019 but Australia outperformed these countries in 
2023.
Poland and Türkiye (Year 5) were not significantly different to Australia in 2019 but outperformed Australia in 
2023, and Macao and Romania outperformed Australia in 2023 (their first TIMSS cycle).
In terms of trends since 1995, 6 countries outperformed Australia in both 1995 and 2023, while England 
performed at a lower level than Australia in 1995 but has improved over the past 28 years to achieve 
significantly higher than Australia in 2023. The Czech Republic and Hungary outperformed Australia in 1995 
but were not significantly different to Australia in 2023, and the United States outperformed Australia in 1995 
but scored significantly lower than Australia in 2023.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
Students who attained the National Proficient Standard
Figure 3.5 shows trends in the percentages of Australian Year 4 students who achieved at each of the 
international benchmarks and the proportion of students who attained the NPS. The proportion of students 
who attained the NPS has increased significantly since 1995, but has not changed since 2019.
The 1995 to 2023 trends show a significant decrease in the percentage of students at and below the Low 
international benchmark and a significant increase in the percentage of students at the High and Advanced 
international benchmarks. The change at the High and Advanced benchmarks is also significant between 
2019 and 2023.
Very high and very low performers
Figure 3.6 shows the trends in the percentages of very high and very low performing students between 
TIMSS 1995 and 2023, by country.
In most countries (9 out of 16) that participated in both TIMSS 1995 and 2023, the percentages of students 
who achieved below the Low benchmark in Year 4 mathematics significantly decreased. Only the United 
States had a higher proportion of very low performers in 2023 than in 1995. Australia’s proportion of very low 
performers decreased by 5 percentage points.
Most countries (13 out of 16 countries) also significantly increased the percentage of students who achieved 
the Advanced benchmark. Only the Czech Republic and the Netherlands had a significant decrease in 
the percentages of students who achieved the Advanced benchmark. Australia’s proportion of very high 
performers increased by 7 percentage points.

FIGURE 3.6 Percentages of very high and very low performers in Year 4 mathematics in 1995 and 2023, by country 
3.2	
Year 4 mathematics results within the national context
Figure 3.7 presents the mean and distribution of Year 4 mathematics performance for each of the Australian 
jurisdictions for TIMSS 2023. Singapore is included for comparison. The jurisdictions are shown in order 
from highest to lowest mean scores.
Read Figure 3.7 in conjunction with Table 3.2, which presents multiple comparisons of mathematics 
performance between jurisdictions and indicates whether a jurisdiction’s performance does, or does not, 
differ significantly from another’s. The mean scores ranged from 542 points in New South Wales to 492 
points in Tasmania.
Students in New South Wales, the Australian Capital Territory and Victoria performed significantly higher than 
students in Western Australia, South Australia, Queensland, the Northern Territory and Tasmania.
Students in Western Australia also performed significantly higher than those in Tasmania.
Western Australia had the largest standard deviation in student performance (97 points), while the Australian 
Capital Territory had the smallest standard deviation in performance (79 points).

Performance at the international benchmarks by jurisdiction
Figure 3.8 shows the percentages of students in each jurisdiction at each of the international benchmarks

Australia as a whole. Singapore and the international median are shown for comparison.

FIGURE 3.8 Percentages of Australian students at the international benchmarks for Year 4 mathematics and 
proportions of students who attained the National Proficient Standard, by jurisdiction
The Australian Capital Territory and New South Wales had the highest proportions of students who attained 
the NPS (both 78%), while Tasmania had the lowest (57%).
The proportions of students who attained the NPS in the Australian Capital Territory, New South Wales 
and Victoria were significantly higher than in Western Australia, Queensland, South Australia, the Northern 
Territory and Tasmania. The proportions of students who attained the NPS in Western Australia and 
Queensland were also significantly higher than in Tasmania.
New South Wales had the highest percentage of very high performers; 18% of students reached the 
Advanced benchmark. The percentage of very high performers in New South Wales was significantly 
higher than in all other jurisdictions. Tasmania had the lowest percentage of very high performers at 5%, 
significantly lower than New South Wales, Victoria, Western Australia, the Australian Capital Territory and 
Queensland. For comparison, 49% of students achieved the Advanced benchmark in Singapore.
The Australian Capital Territory had the lowest percentage of very low performers; only 6% of students did 
not reach the Low benchmark. Tasmania and the Northern Territory had 15% of very low performers. In 
Singapore, only 2% of students were below the Low benchmark. The percentage of very low performers in 
the Australian Capital Territory and New South Wales was significantly lower than in all other jurisdictions, 
except for Victoria. The percentage of very low performers in Victoria was significantly lower than in Western 
Australia and Queensland.

FIGURE 3.9 (continued) Trends in Year 4 mathematics achievement within Australia, 1995–2023, by jurisdiction

32
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
The results show that there were significant changes in Year 4 mathematics achievement scores for some 
jurisdictions over time. Since 1995, New South Wales increased significantly by 46 points, Western Australia 
by 28 points, Victoria and South Australia by 24 points and Queensland by 23 points. Since 2019, Northern 
Territory increased significantly by 36 points, and New South Wales by 20 points. In contrast, Tasmania 
decreased significantly by 22 points.
Figure 3.10 shows the proportions of students who attained the NPS, as well as the proportions of very high 
and very low performers in Year 4 mathematics, for each Australian jurisdiction for all TIMSS cycles from 
1995 through to 2023.
The proportion of students who attained the NPS significantly increased between 1995 and 2023 in 
4 jurisdictions: New South Wales, Queensland, South Australia and Western Australia. Between 2019 and 
2023, only the Northern Territory had a significant increase in the proportion of students who attained the 
NPS; Tasmania showed a significant decrease in this time.
The percentages of very high performers increased significantly between 1995 and 2023 in New South Wales 
(by 11 percentage points), Western Australia (by 7 percentage points), Victoria (by 6 percentage points), and 
Queensland (by 4 percentage points). However, there were no significant changes since 2019.
Since 1995, the percentages of very low performers decreased significantly between 1995 and 2023 in New 
South Wales and South Australia (by 7 percentage points each). Since 2019, the percentage of very low 
achievers decreased significantly in the Northern Territory (by 13 percentage points).

3.3	
Year 4 mathematics results for different demographic groups
3.3.1	
Results by gender
Figure 3.11 shows international Year 4 mathematics achievement for male and female students. The 
average achievement is shown separately for females and males, as well as the differences between the 
averages. Differences are shown by a bar that indicates the size and direction (in favour of male or female 
students) and whether the difference was statistically significant (indicated by a darkened bar). Countries are 
presented in order of the highest to lowest difference between females and males in average achievement.
Notably, male students outperformed female students in 40 of the 58 participating countries. In contrast, 
South Africa was the only country where females significantly outperformed males (by 29 points). Overall, 
there was an 11-point difference in achievement between females and males (the international average 
scores were 498 and 508, respectively).
Australia, along with France had the widest gap in favour of males with a 23-score point difference.
Figure 3.12 shows the mean score and distribution of mathematics achievement for male and female Year 
4 students in Australia. It shows the 23-point difference in the mean score of male and female students. 
However, the variability in scores was slightly greater for Year 4 male students (SD = 93) than for Year 4 
female students (SD = 88).

Performance at the international benchmarks by gender
Figure 3.13 presents the percentages of female and male Year 4 students in Australia at each of the 
international benchmarks for mathematics as well as the proportions of students who attained the NPS.

Figure 3.14 shows the trends in the mathematics achievement of Year 4 male and female students in
