3.3.2	
Results by books in the home
This section presents Year 4 Australian students’ mathematics achievement according to the number of 
books in the home. For more information about this variable, please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Figure 3.17 provides the percentage of students in each category and their average achievement scores. 
Fifty-four per cent of Australian Year 4 students reported having an average number of books and 16% 
reported having many books at home. The students who had many books in the home also had the highest 
levels of achievement and scored 12 points, on average, higher than students who had an average number 
of books in the home, and 64 points higher than those who had a few books in the home. All differences in 
scale scores were significant.
Figure 3.17 also shows the distribution of scores in mathematics achievement of Year 4 students for each 
category of books at home. As indicated by the standard deviation, the spread of scores is greater for those 
students with many books in the home compared to students with a few books or an average number of 
books in the home.

FIGURE 3.17 Mean scores and distribution of Year 4 mathematics achievement within Australia, by number of books in 
the home 
Figure 3.18 presents the percentage of students at each of the international benchmarks for mathematics as 
well as the proportions of students who attained the NPS, by the number of books in the home.
Of those students who reported having many books in the home, 80% attained the NPS, compared to 79% of 
students with an average number of books in the home and 56% of those with only a few books in the home. 
The proportion of students with a few books in the home that attained the NPS was significantly lower than 
that of students with many or an average number of books in the home.
In terms of very high performing students, of those students who reported having many books in the home, 
23% achieved the Advanced benchmark, while only 15% of students with an average number of books and 
just 6% with a few books in the home attained this level of achievement. These differences are all significant.
At the other end of the scale, 7% of students who reported having many books in the home and 5% with an 
average number of books in the home did not achieve even the Low international benchmark, significantly 
lower than the 17% of students who reported having a few books in the home.

3.3.3	
Results by First Nations background
‘Supporting Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander learners to reach their full potential’ is one of the areas for 
action identified in the Alice Springs (Mparntwe) education declaration (Education Council, 2019, p.16). As 
such, educational outcomes for young students with First Nations background are a key area of interest for 
the education community. This section presents Australian students’ mathematics achievement according 
to whether students identified as being First Nations Australian. For more information about this variable, 
please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Ten per cent of the TIMSS sample at Year 4 identified as being a First Nations Australian. As shown in Figure 
3.19, First Nations students attained an average score in mathematics of 462 points.
Figure 3.19 also presents the distribution of Year 4 achievement. As is indicated by the standard deviation, 
the spread of scores for First Nations students was very similar to that of non-First Nations students.
Figure 3.20 presents the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks for mathematics 
as well as the proportions of First Nations students who attained the NPS.
Forty-five per cent of First Nations students achieved the NPS, while 3% were very high performers and 23% 
were very low performers.
All differences with non-First Nations students were significant.

Chapter 3 Australia’s Year 4 mathematics results

3.3.4	
Results by language spoken at home
The frequency with which English is spoken at home is a factor that has been associated with Year 4 
mathematics achievement in past cycles of TIMSS. Students who come from homes in which English is not 
spoken frequently have had less exposure to the language of instruction and test, which can place them 
at a disadvantage. However, this variable can be difficult to interpret, as the language-spoken group is very 
diverse. While it includes those migrants to Australia who have limited English language skills at home, it 
also includes a substantial proportion of students who are economic immigrants to Australia whose main 
language at home may not be English, but this is not a barrier to their understanding of mathematics (in 
particular).
This section presents Year 4 mathematics achievement according to whether a language other than English 
was spoken as the main language at home. For more information about this variable, please refer to the 
Reader’s guide.
Figure 3.22 shows that 21% of students in the TIMSS Year 4 sample indicated they did not speak English at 
home always or almost always. Students who did not speak English at home always or almost always scored 
significantly higher than those who did speak English at home.
Figure 3.22 also shows the distribution of scores for students by their language background. As is indicated 
by the standard deviation, the spread of scores for students who spoke a language other than English at 
home (SD = 98) was larger than that of students with an English-speaking background (SD = 89).

FIGURE 3.22 Mean scores and distribution of Year 4 mathematics achievement within Australia, by language spoken 
at home
Figure 3.23 shows the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks and the proportions 
of students who attained the NPS, by language background.
There was little difference between students who spoke a language other than English at home and students 
who spoke English at home always or almost always in terms of the proportion that attained the NPS and the 
percentage of very low performers.
However, 19% of students who spoke a language other than English at home compared to 12% of students 
who spoke English at home were very high performers. This was a significant difference.

3.3.5	
Results by geographic location of the school
Past cycles of TIMSS have found that students who attended schools in remote or regional areas of Australia 
are often at an educational disadvantage compared to students at schools in major cities. This section 
presents Australian students’ mathematics achievement according to the geographic location of the school.
TIMSS 2023 results by school geographic location are reported using the 3 broad categories of the 
Australian Bureau of Statistics Australian statistical geography standard remoteness structure (ABS, 2011). 
For more information about these variables, please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Figure 3.24 shows the Year 4 mathematics performance of students from schools according to geographic 
location of the school. Students who attended school in remote areas made up only 1% of the Year 4 TIMSS 
sample, while those who attended school in major cities made up 74% of the sample. On average, students 
who attended schools in major cities scored 28 points higher than students who attended schools in regional 
areas and 30 points higher than students at schools in remote areas. Only the difference between students 
in schools in major cities and students in schools in regional areas was significant (due to the large standard 
error for the remote schools category).
Figure 3.25 shows the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks and the proportions 
of students who attained the NPS, according to geographic location of the school.
Seventy-five per cent of students who attended schools in major cities met the NPS compared to 64% of 
students in regional schools, and this difference was significant. The percentage of very high performers 
was also significantly higher for students who attended schools in major cities than students at schools in 
regional areas. There were no significant differences in the percentage of very low performers.

Chapter 3 Australia’s Year 4 mathematics results

3.4	
Year 4 mathematics results in the TIMSS content  
and cognitive domains
As noted in Chapter 2, the TIMSS mathematics assessment can be described in terms of content and 
cognitive domains. The content domains outline the subject matter to be assessed and include Number, 
Measurement and geometry, and Data at Year 4. The cognitive domains detail the thinking processes that 
students need to use. These domains are Knowing, Applying and Reasoning. Each item is associated with a 
single content domain and a single cognitive domain. This allows student performance to be described in 
terms of achievement in each of the domains.
To allow comparisons of student achievement across the domains, the content and cognitive achievement 
scales at each year level were constructed to have the same average level of difficulty. The tables in this 
section present the average achievement in each of the Year 4 mathematics content and cognitive domains 
for Australia as a whole, for each of the Australian jurisdictions, for males and females, and by First Nations 
background. These tables also show trends for Australia since 2007 in the content and cognitive domains.
3.4.1	
Mathematics content domains: Number, Measurement and geometry, 
and Data
Table 3.3 provides the Year 4 achievement scores in the mathematics content domains for Australia, the 
jurisdictions, gender and First Nations background.
Australian Year 4 students performed relatively higher in Data and relatively lower in Number. These 
differences were statistically significant for Australia as a whole. The score for Measurement and geometry 
was not significantly different to the average overall mathematics score.
This relative strength in Data was found for all jurisdictions apart from Northern Territory, for male and 
female students, and for First Nations and non-First Nations students.
The relative weakness in Number was found only for Queensland and Western Australia, female students and 
non-First Nations students.
The score for Measurement and geometry was not significantly different to the average overall mathematics 
score across all groups.
There was a significant difference in performance in all 3 domains between male and female students 
(where male students outperformed female students). There was a significant difference in performance 
in all domains between First Nations and non-First Nations students (where non-First Nations students 
performed higher).

3.4.2	
Mathematics cognitive domains: Knowing, Applying, and Reasoning
Table 3.5 presents the Year 4 achievement scores in the mathematics cognitive domains for Australia, the 
jurisdictions, gender and First Nations background.
Australian Year 4 students performed relatively higher in the Knowing cognitive domain and relatively lower 
in Applying. These differences were statistically significant for Australia as a whole. The average score in 
Reasoning was not different to the Australian average overall.
This relative strength in Knowing was found only for New South Wales, male students and non-First Nations

The relative weakness in Applying was found only for male students and non-First Nations students.
There was a significant difference in performance in all 3 domains between male and female students 
(where male students outperformed female students in Knowing, Applying and Reasoning). There was a 
significant difference in performance in all domains between First Nations and non-First Nations students 
(where non-First Nations students performed higher).

CHAPTER 4
AUSTRALIA’S YEAR 8 
MATHEMATICS RESULTS
In this report, the focus is on differences that are statistically significant (in other words, are unlikely 
to have occurred by chance). Where the commentary states that there was a difference between sets 
of numbers, whether these were scores, percentages or percentage point differences, it means that 
the difference satisfied this condition. Where the commentary states that there was no difference, or 
where no comment is made regarding a possible comparison, it indicates that the difference was not 
statistically significant.
Due to large standard errors for some results, differences that appear numerically larger may not be 
statistically significant. For more information about standard errors and statistical significance, please 
refer to the Reader’s Guide.
4.1	
Year 8 mathematics results within the international context
This section reports the TIMSS 2023 mathematics results as average scores and distributions on the TIMSS 
Year 8 mathematics scale (please see the Reader’s guide for more information about the achievement 
scales).
Australian Year 8 students achieved a mean score of 509 points in mathematics. This performance was 
lower than students in 8 countries (Singapore, Chinese Taipei, Korea, Japan, Hong Kong, England, Ireland and 
the Czech Republic); not significantly different to students in 8 countries (Sweden, Lithuania, Austria, Türkiye, 
Hungary, Finland, Norway and Italy) and higher than students in 25 countries (including the United States).
Figure 4.1 provides the mean scores achieved by students in each country, the standard error and confidence 
interval of each mean and the standard deviation (an indication in the variability of individual scores around 
the mean). Countries are positioned from highest to lowest level of achievement; however, this is not a 
simple ranking, as the differences between countries may not be statistically significant. The shading 
indicates whether the score for a given country was significantly different to Australia. To determine whether 
or not differences between other countries were statistically significant, please refer to the TIMSS 2023 
international results in mathematics and science (von Davier, et al., 2024).

FIGURE 4.1 Mean scores and distribution of Year 8 mathematics achievement, by country
Singapore, Chinese Taipei and Korea were the top-performing countries of TIMSS 2023 with 605, 602, and 
596 points, respectively. The scores for these countries were not significantly different to each other but were 
significantly higher than those of all other countries. Japan was the next highest with 595 points, then Hong 
Kong with 575 points. The mean score for these 5 countries exceeded the High benchmark of 550 points.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
4.1.1	
Performance at the international benchmarks
In addition to analysing performance according to mean scores, the international benchmarks give further 
beneficial insight into student achievement.
Figure 4.2 shows the percentages of students in each country at each of the international benchmarks. The 
countries are ordered by the percentages of students who reached the Intermediate international benchmark, 
which is the National Proficient Standard (NPS) set for TIMSS mathematics in Australia (please see the 
Reader’s guide for more information about the NPS).
Students who attained the National Proficient Standard
In TIMSS 2023, 64% of Australian students attained the NPS in Year 8 mathematics, compared to the 
international median of 55%.
In Japan, 92% of students achieved at least the Intermediate international benchmark. There was similar 
achievement in Singapore and Chinese Taipei (both 89%), Korea (88%), and Hong Kong (85%).
Very high performers
In Australia, 11% of Year 8 students were very high performers in mathematics, compared to the international 
median of 7%.
The countries with the highest average mathematics achievement were also the countries with the largest 
percentages of students who reached the Advanced benchmark. In Singapore, 46% of Year 8 students were 
very high performers. In Chinese Taipei, 44% were high performers, followed by Korea with 40%. In Japan and 
Hong Kong, the proportions were 37% and 32%, respectively.
Australia was one of only 11 countries that had 10% or more of very high performers. Four countries (Saudi 
Arabia, Palestinian National Authority, Jordan and Morocco) had fewer than 1% of students who attained the 
Advanced benchmark.
Very low performers
In Australia, 13% of Year 8 students were very low performers in mathematics, compared to the international 
median of 19%.
In Japan, only 1% of students did not achieve the Low international benchmark. In Singapore, Chinese Taipei 
and Korea, 3% were very low performers.
The proportion of very low performers was less than 20% in most countries. However, in 13 countries, the 
proportion was more than 40% and in 7 countries (Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, Palestinian National Authority, 
Jordan, South Africa, Brazil, Morocco), it was more than 50%.

4.1.2	
Trends in Year 8 mathematics achievement
Looking at the overall trends in Year 8 mathematics achievement during the 1995–2023 period, there were 
more countries with increases in achievement rather than decreases.
Since 2019:
	
z 3 out of 34 countries increased in average mathematics achievement.
	
z 14 out of 34 countries decreased in average mathematics achievement.
	
z Romania had the greatest increase in achievement (by 17 points) and Bahrain had the largest decrease 
(by 55 points).
	
z Australia’s score did not change significantly.
Since 1995:
	
z 7 out of 17 countries increased in average mathematics achievement.
	
z 4 out of 17 countries decreased in average mathematics achievement.
	
z Lithuania had the greatest increase in achievement (by 42 points) and France had the largest decrease (by 
51 points).
	
z Australia’s score did not change significantly.
Patterns of achievement
Figure 4.3 graphs trends in Year 8 mathematics achievement for selected countries with comparable data 
from TIMSS 1995 to 2023. The comparison countries are the United States, England, New Zealand, Singapore 
and Hungary, which have participated in all cycles since 1995. The patterns of achievement through the 8 
cycles of TIMSS have varied considerably across countries.5
Between 1995 and 2023, the following key trends are noted:
	
z Up until the 2019 cycle, the United States was the only country in this group whose mean scores have 
consistently increased or stayed statistically the same since 1995; however, their mean score for TIMSS 
2023 significantly decreased by 27 points since 2019.
	
z England recorded the greatest improvement from 1995 to 2023, with an increase of 27 points over this 
28-year period.
	
z Hungary had the greatest decline in mean achievement from 1995 to 2023 with a decrease of 21 points. 
Hungary had a slight rise in 1999 but continued to decline until 2011 when it experienced a significant 
decrease. In the 2015 and 2019 cycles, Hungary had small increases but declined again in 2023.
	
z New Zealand’s mean scores fell between 1995 and 1999, but then held steady until another fall in 2019.
	
z Singapore’s scores fell between 1995 and 2007 and then rose to peak in 2015; Singapore’s mean score 
in 2023 is similar to its 1995 score. Singapore has remained the highest performing country in this 
comparison group for all cycles since 1995.
From 1995 to 2007, Australia’s mathematics achievement declined, but had its greatest improvement in 
2019. However, in 2023 the average scale score dropped and fell level with the 1995 score. Australia’s 
average scale score for TIMSS 2023 was significantly higher than the 2007 cycle.
Figure 4.4 shows the distribution of mathematics achievement for Australian Year 8 students across the 
cycles. The spread of mathematics achievement scores for Australian Year 8 students has widened since 
1995.
5	
The Australian Year 8 sample that participated in 1999 was not comparable to that in other cycles, so no trend results are provided for Australia at Year 8 in 1999. Other 
countries may not have data for certain cycles if they did not participate in that cycle or had other inconsistencies that do not allow for trends.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
The scores for both England and Ireland were not significantly different to Australia in 2019, but both 
countries outperformed Australia in 2023. The United States and Israel performed at a similar level to 
Australia in 2019, but both scored significantly lower than Australia in 2023. Sweden, Norway, Türkiye and 
Italy scored significantly lower than Australia in 2019 but performed at a similar level to Australia in 2023.
In terms of long-term trends, Singapore, Korea, Japan, the Czech Republic and Hong Kong all scored at a 
significantly higher level to Australia in 1995 and did so again in 2023. Chinese Taipei has outperformed 
Australia in each cycle since it first participated in 2003.
England scored lower than Australia in 1995 but has improved over the past 28 years to outperform Australia 
in 2023. Lithuania scored lower than Australia in 1995 but has improved to perform similarly to Australia in 
2023. Ireland performed at a similar level to Australia in 1995, but outperformed Australia in 2023. Sweden 
and Hungary outperformed Australia in 1995 but performed at a similar level to Australia in 2023, and France 
outperformed Australia in 1995, but was outperformed by Australia in 2023.
Very high and very low performers
Figure 4.5 shows the trends in the percentages of very high and very low performing students between 
TIMSS 1995 and 2023, by country.
For 11 out of 17 countries that participated in both TIMSS 1995 and 2023, the percentages of students 
who achieved the Advanced benchmark in Year 8 mathematics significantly increased. For 3 countries, the 
percentages of students who achieved the Advanced benchmark decreased. The proportion of very high 
performers increased by 4 percentage points in Australia.
Over the same period, 3 of the 17 countries had significant decreases in the percentages of students who 
performed below the Low benchmark. In 5 countries (Singapore, Czech Republic, Sweden, Hungary and 
France), the percentages of students who performed below the Low benchmark significantly increased. The 
proportion of very low performers did not change significantly in Australia.

Students who attained the National Proficient Standard
Figure 4.6 shows the trends in the percentages of Australian Year 8 students who achieved at each of the 
international benchmarks and the proportion of students who attained the NPS. The proportion of students 
who attained the NPS has not changed significantly over this time.

benchmark has increased significantly since 2019 (by 3 percentage points).
In contrast, since 1995 there has been significant increase (of 4 percentage points) in the percentage of 
students performing above the Advanced benchmark.

4.2	
Year 8 mathematics results within the national context
Figure 4.7 presents the means and distribution of Year 8 mathematics performance for each of the 
Australian jurisdictions for TIMSS 2023. Singapore is included for comparison. The jurisdictions are shown in 
order from highest to lowest mean scores.
Read Figure 4.7 in conjunction with Table 4.2, which presents multiple comparisons of mathematics 
performance between jurisdictions and indicates whether a jurisdiction’s performance does, or does not, 
differ significantly from another’s. The mean scores ranged from 525 points in the Australian Capital Territory 
to 477 points in the Northern Territory.
Students in the Australian Capital Territory and Western Australia performed significantly higher than 
students in Queensland, South Australia, Tasmania and the Northern Territory.
Students in Victoria performed significantly higher than students in South Australia, Tasmania and the 
Northern Territory, while students in New South Wales performed significantly higher than students in the 
Northern Territory.
New South Wales had the largest standard deviation in student performance (99 points), while South 
Australia had the smallest standard deviation in performance (83 points).

4.2.1	
Performance at the international benchmarks by jurisdiction
Figure 4.8 shows the percentages of students in each jurisdiction at each of the international benchmarks 
for Year 8 mathematics, as well as the proportion who attained the NPS, along with the percentages for 
Australia as a whole. Singapore and the international median are shown for comparison.
The highest proportion of students who attained the NPS was in the Australian Capital Territory (71%); 
the lowest was in the Northern Territory (52%). The Australian Capital Territory and Western Australia had 
significantly higher proportions of students who attained the NPS than South Australia, Tasmania and the 
Northern Territory. Western Australia also had a significantly higher proportion of students who attained the 
NPS than Queensland. Victoria had a significantly higher proportion of students who attained the NPS than 
South Australia and the Northern Territory.
The Australian Capital Territory, Western Australia and New South Wales had the highest percentages of very 
high performers (13%). The Northern Territory had the lowest percentage of very high performers, at 3%. 
For comparison, 46% of students achieved the Advanced benchmark in Singapore. The percentage of very 
high performers was significantly higher in the Australian Capital Territory, New South Wales and Western 
Australia than in Queensland, South Australia, Tasmania and the Northern Territory. The percentage of very 
high performers was significantly higher in Victoria than in South Australia, Tasmania and the Northern 
Territory, and the percentage of very high performers was significantly higher in Queensland than in the 
Northern Territory.

The Australian Capital Territory and Western Australia had the lowest percentages of very low performers; 
only 9% of students did not reach the Low benchmark. Tasmania and the Northern Territory had 20% of 
very low performers. In Singapore, only 3% of students were below the Low benchmark. The percentage of 
very low performers was significantly higher in Tasmania than in the Australian Capital Territory, Western 
Australia and Victoria.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
The results show that there was little significant change in scores for most of the jurisdictions in 
mathematics achievement at Year 8 over time. In terms of change since 1995, South Australia had a 
significant decrease of 18 points. Since 2019, New South Wales was the only jurisdiction to have a significant 
change, with a decrease of 25 points.
Figure 4.10 shows the proportions of students who attained the NPS, as well as the percentages of very high 
and very low performers in Year 8 mathematics, for each Australian jurisdiction for all TIMSS cycles from 
1995 through to 2023.
There was no significant change in the proportions of students who attained the NPS, as well as the 
percentages of very high and very low performers in Year 8 mathematics since 2019.
In terms of change in the proportions of students who attained the NPS since 1995, only South Australia 
showed a significant decrease (by 13 percentage points).
There were increases in very low performers between 1995 and 2023. South Australia increased by 7 
percentage points, Western Australia by 5 percentage points and Tasmania by 8 percentage points.
The percentages of very high performers increased significantly in Victoria (by 7 percentage points) over 
this time.

4.3	
Year 8 mathematics results for different demographic groups
4.3.1	
Results by gender
Figure 4.11 shows international Year 8 mathematics achievement for male and female students. The average 
achievement is shown separately for females and males, as well as the differences between the averages. 
Differences are shown by a bar indicating the size and direction (in favour of males or females) and whether 
the difference was statistically significant (indicated by a darkened bar). Countries are presented in order of 
the highest to lowest difference between females and males in average achievement.
Overall, there was a 6-point difference in achievement in favour of males. For 17 countries there were no 
significant gender differences in Year 8 mathematics achievement. Of the remaining countries, 4 countries 
(Palestinian National Authority, Oman, Bahrain and South Africa) had a difference that favoured female 
students and 21 countries, including Australia, had significant differences that favoured males. In 3 countries 
(Brazil, Chile and England) the gender differences were more than 20 points in favour of males.
Figure 4.12 shows the mean score and distribution of mathematics achievement for male and female Year 
8 students in Australia. It shows that there was a significant difference in the mean score between male and 
female students, with males performing 13 points higher. However, the variability in scores was greater for 
Year 8 males (SD = 95) than for Year 8 females (SD = 89). This is seen in the distribution of scores where 
the highest performing male students achieved at a slightly higher level than the highest performing female 
students (but there was little difference at the lower end of the distribution).

FIGURE 4.12 Mean scores and distribution of Year 8 mathematics achievement within Australia, by gender
Performance at the international benchmarks by gender
Figure 4.13 presents the percentages of female and male Year 8 students at each of the international 
benchmarks for mathematics as well as the proportions of students who attained the NPS.
There was a significant 5 percentage point difference between males and females in the proportion of 
students who attained the NPS. Reflecting what was seen in the distribution graph in Figure 4.12, there was 
also a significant difference in the percentage of very high performers; significantly more male students 
achieved the Advanced international benchmark (by 4 percentage points). There was no significant 
difference in terms of the percentages of very low performers.

Trends in mathematics achievement by gender
Figure 4.14 shows the trends in the mathematics achievement of Year 8 male and female students in 
Australia from TIMSS 1995 to 2023. The gap between male and female students widened to a significant 
15 points in 2007 and then closed again, with no significant difference in 2019. In 2023, the gap widened 
to a significant 13 points, favouring males. In 2023, mathematics achievement for female students had 
significantly declined by 13 points since 2019.

4.3.2	
Results by books in the home
This section presents Year 8 Australian students’ mathematics achievement according to the number of 
books in the home. For more information about this variable, please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Figure 4.17 provides the percentages of students in each category and their average achievement score. 
Forty-seven per cent of Australian Year 8 students reported having an average number of books and 18% 
reported having many books at home. The students who had many books in the home also had the highest 
levels of achievement, and scored 22 points, on average, higher than students who had an average number 
of books in the home, and 82 points higher than those who had a few books in the home. The students who 
had an average number of books in the home scored higher than those who had a few books in the home (by 
60 points). All differences in scale scores were significant.

FIGURE 4.17 Mean scores and distribution of Year 8 mathematics achievement within Australia, by number of books in 
the home
Figure 4.17 also shows the distribution of scores in mathematics achievement of Year 8 students for each 
category of books at home. As is indicated by the standard deviation, those who reported having a few 
books in the home had the widest range of scores (SD = 91), while those who had many books or an average 
number of books at home had the narrowest (SD = 85).
Figure 4.18 presents the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks for mathematics 
as well as the proportions of students who attained the NPS, by the number of books in the home.
Of those students who reported having many books in the home, 81% attained the NPS, compared to 73% of 
students with an average number of books in the home and 45% of those with only a few books in the home. 
These differences were all significant.
In terms of very high performing students, 18% of students who reported having many books in the home 
achieved the Advanced benchmark. Only 13% of students in the average number of books category and just 
5% in the a few books category attained this level of achievement. These differences were all significant.
At the other end of the scale 5% of students who reported having many books in the home and 7% with an 
average number of books in the home did not achieve even the Low international benchmark, significantly 
lower than the 24% of students who reported having a few books in the home.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
4.3.3	
Results by level of parental education
Parental education has also been found to be strongly related to student achievement. This section presents 
Australian students’ mathematics achievement according to the level of parental education. For more 
information about this variable, please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Figure 4.19 shows the mean scores and associated standard errors in mathematics for Year 8 Australian 
students according to the highest level of education attained by either parent. Just over half of the students 
(55%) who responded to this question said that at least one of their parents had a university degree, while 
only 9% responded that neither parent had completed upper secondary education. As can be seen, the mean 
score increases as the level of parental education increases; students who had at least one parent with a 
university degree achieved a mean score that was a substantial 124 points higher than students whose 
parents had not completed secondary school. They also scored 71 points higher than the mean score for 
students for whom the highest level of parental education was completion of secondary school and 46 
points higher than students whose parents had completed a Technical and Further Education qualification. 
All differences were statistically significant.
Figure 4.19 also shows the spread of scores in mathematics achievement for Year 8 students for the 
different parental education groups. As is indicated by the standard deviation, the spread of scores was 
similar for all groups.
Figure 4.20 presents the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks for mathematics, 
as well as the proportions of students who attained the NPS, according to level of parental education.
Figure 4.20 shows that the proportion of students who attained the NPS increases with the level of parental 
education. The proportion of students who had at least one parent with a university degree and attained 
the NPS was 80% compared to 26% for students whose parents did not complete secondary school. All 
differences between levels of parental education were significant.
Similarly, the percentage of very high performers increases with the level of parental education; 19% 
of students who had at least one parent with a university degree achieved the Advanced benchmark, 
compared to 2% of students whose parents did not complete secondary school. The percentage of very high 
performers was significantly higher for students who had at least one parent with a university degree than 
for all other groups; while the percentage of very high performers was significantly higher for students whose 
parents completed post-secondary education but not university than for students whose parents did not 
complete secondary school.
Conversely, the percentage of very low performers decreases with the level of parental education; 42% of 
students whose parents did not complete secondary school, compared to 5% of students who had at least 
one parent with a university degree. All differences between levels of parental education were significant.

FIGURE 4.20 Percentages of Australian students at the international benchmarks for Year 8 mathematics, by parental

4.3.4	
Results by educational resources in the home
The presence or absence of educational resources in the home expresses potential advantage or 
disadvantage for students that may reflect the ability of parents to provide materially for their children or 
indicate differences in practical and psychological support for academic achievement. This section presents 
Australian students’ mathematics achievement according to the number of educational resources in the 
home. For more information about this variable, please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Australia recorded one of the highest proportions of students who had many resources at home and had 
42% of students in this category, similar to Cyprus, Finland, Hungary, Sweden (all 42%) and Japan (43%). 
Only Korea and Norway had higher percentages of students in this category (56% and 47%, respectively). 
Australia’s percentage of students with only a few resources at home (13%) was also quite low by 
international standards. Korea had the lowest percentage of students with only a few resources at home 
(10%), Israel had 11% and Finland, Japan and Norway each had 12%. On average, internationally, 26% of 
students had many resources at home, 45% had some resources and 28% had few resources at home.
Figure 4.21 shows that, in Australia, Year 8 students who had many resources in the home performed at a 
significantly higher level than those who had some resources, who again performed at a significantly higher 
level than those who had few resources. This pattern was found on average, to hold across participating 
countries.
Australian Year 8 students who had many resources achieved a mean score 55 points higher than those who 
had some resources, whose average achievement was 55 points higher than those with few resources at 
home. Therefore, the average achievement of those with many resources was 110 points more than those 
with few resources. All differences were statistically significant.
Figure 4.21 also shows the distribution in mathematics achievement for Australian students for each of the 
categories of the Home educational resources scale. As is indicated by the standard deviation, the spread 
of scores is smallest for those with many resources (SD = 83), while those with some resources or few 
resources at home had a similar spread of scores.

Figure 4.22 presents the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks for mathematics 
as well as the proportions of students who attained the NPS for each of the categories of the Home

The proportion of students with many resources who attained the NPS was significantly higher than students

NPS was significantly higher than that of students with few resources (by 26 percentage points).

higher for students with some resources than students with few resources (by 5 percentage points).

students with some resources (by 9 percentage points) and significantly lower than that for students with 
few resources (by 31 percentage points). The percentage of very low performers was also significantly lower 
for students with some resources than students with few resources (by 22 percentage points).

4.3.5	
Results by First Nations background
‘Supporting Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander learners to reach their full potential’ is one of the areas for 
action identified in the Alice Springs (Mparntwe) education declaration (Education Council, 2019, p.16). As 
such, educational outcomes for young First Nations Australians are a key area of interest for the education 
community. This section presents Australian students’ mathematics achievement according to whether 
students identified as being a First Nations Australian. For more information about this variable, please refer 
to the Reader’s guide.
Seven per cent of the TIMSS sample at Year 8 identified as being a First Nations person. As shown in 
Figure 4.23, First Nations students attained an average score in mathematics of 435 points. Figure 4.23 
also presents the distribution of Year 8 achievement. As is indicated by the standard deviation, the spread 
of scores for First Nations students is slightly narrower (SD = 83) than the spread of scores for non-First 
Nations students (SD = 91).

4.3.6	
Results by language spoken at home
The frequency with which English is spoken at home is a factor that has been associated with Year 8 
mathematics achievement in past cycles of TIMSS. Students who come from homes in which English is 
not spoken frequently have less exposure to the language of instruction and test, which can place them at 
a disadvantage. However, this variable can be difficult to interpret, as the language-spoken group is very 
diverse. While it includes those migrants to Australia who have limited English language skills at home, 
there is also a substantial proportion of students in this group who are economic immigrants to Australia, 
and, while English may not be the main language at home, this is no barrier to their understanding of 
mathematics, in particular.
This section presents Year 8 mathematics achievement according to whether a language other than English 
was spoken as the main language at home. For more information about this variable, please refer to the 
Reader’s guide.
Figure 4.26 shows that 10% of students in the TIMSS Year 8 sample indicated that they did not speak English 
at home always or almost always. There was no significant difference in the average score in mathematics 
according to language background.
Figure 4.26 also shows the distribution of scores for students by their language background. As is indicated 
by the standard deviation, the spread of scores for students who spoke a language other than English at 
home (SD = 108) was larger than that of students with an English-speaking background (SD = 91).
Figure 4.27 presents the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks for mathematics 
as well as the proportions of students who attained the NPS by language background.
There was no significant difference in the proportion of students who attained the NPS according to the 
language background.
However, the percentage of students who spoke a language other than English at home who were very 
high performers was significantly higher than that of students with an English-speaking background (by 9 
percentage points).
There was no significant difference in the percentage of very low performers according to the language 
background.

4.3.7	
Results by geographic location of the school
Past cycles of TIMSS have found that students who attended schools in remote or regional areas of Australia 
are often at an educational disadvantage compared to students at schools in major cities. This section 
presents Australian students’ mathematics achievement according to the geographic location of the school, 
reported using the 3 broad categories (major cities, regional, remote) of the Australian Bureau of Statistics 
Australian statistical geography standard remoteness structure (ABS, 2011). For more information about this 
variable, please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Figure 4.28 shows the performance of students from schools according to the geographic location of the 
school. Students who attended schools in remote areas made up only 1% of the Year 8 TIMSS sample, while 
those who attended school in major cities made up 74% of the sample. On average, students at schools in 
major cities scored 34 points higher than students who attended schools in regional areas, and 65 points 
higher than students in remote schools. Both differences were significant, while the difference between 
schools in regional and remote areas was not significant.
Figure 4.28 also provides the spread of scores for mathematics achievement by geographic location of 
school. As is indicated by the standard deviation, the spread of scores for students who attended schools in 
major cities and regional areas (SD = 92) was larger than for students who attended schools in remote areas 
(SD=73).

Figure 4.29 shows the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks and the proportions

The proportion of students who attended schools in major cities and attained the NPS was higher than 
students who attended schools in regional areas (by 12 percentage points) and also higher than students 
who attended schools in remote areas (by 30 percentage points). The difference between regional and 
remote areas was not significant (due to the large standard error for the remote areas category).
The proportion of students who achieved the Advanced benchmark was 12% for students in schools in major 
cities, compared to 6% in regional and less than 1% in remote schools. All differences were statistically 
significant.

At the same time, while only 11% of students in schools in major cities failed to achieve the Low international

4.4	
Year 8 mathematics results in the TIMSS content  
and cognitive domains
As noted in Chapter 2, the TIMSS mathematics assessment can be described in terms of content and 
cognitive domains. The content domains outline the subject matter to be assessed and include Number, 
Algebra, Geometry and measurement, and Data and probability at Year 8. The cognitive domains detail the 
thinking processes that students need to use. These are Knowing, Applying and Reasoning. Each item is 
associated with a single content domain and a single cognitive domain. This allows student performance to 
be described in terms of achievement in each of the domains.
To allow comparisons of student achievement across the domains, the content and cognitive achievement 
scales at each year level were constructed to have the same average level of difficulty. The following tables 
present the average achievement in each of the Year 8 mathematics content and cognitive domains for 
Australia as a whole, for each of the Australian jurisdictions, for males and females, and by First Nations 
background students. These tables also show trends for Australia since 2007 in the content and cognitive 
domains.
4.4.1	
Mathematics content domains: Number, Algebra, Geometry and 
measurement, and Data and probability
Table 4.3 provides the Year 8 achievement scores in the mathematics content domains for Australia, the 
jurisdictions, by gender and by First Nations background.
Australian Year 8 students performed relatively better in Data and probability, but relatively less well in 
Number and Algebra. These differences were statistically significant for Australia as a whole.
The relatively poorer performance for Algebra and relatively stronger performance for Data and probability 
held for all jurisdictions, for male and female students and for First Nations students (although not all 
differences were statistically significant due to larger standard errors).
Only students in Queensland and female students had significantly lower performance in Number compared 
to mathematics overall. Male students had significantly lower performance in Geometry and measurement.
There was a significant difference between males and females in their performance in Number but not in the 
other content domains.
Non-First Nations students performed significantly higher than First Nations students in all content domains.
Table 4.4 shows the trends in achievement for the content domains for Australia as a whole. Performance in 
Number declined by 17 points since 2019, whereas performance in Algebra, Geometry and measurement, and 
Data and probability was not significantly different to 2019.

4.4.2	
Mathematics cognitive domains: Knowing, Applying, and Reasoning
Table 4.5 provides the Year 8 achievement scores in the mathematics cognitive domains for Australia, the 
jurisdictions, by gender and by First Nations background.
Australian Year 8 students had similar performance in the Knowing cognitive domain and Applying but 
performed significantly lower in Reasoning.
However, at the jurisdiction level and for the demographic groups, the relatively lower performance in 
Reasoning was significant only in New South Wales and Western Australia, males, females and non-First 
Nations students.
There was a significant difference in performance in all 3 domains between male and female students 
(where male students outperformed female students).
There was a significant difference in performance in all domains between First Nations and non-First Nations 
students (where non-First Nations students performed higher).
Table 4.6 shows the trends in achievement for the cognitive domains for Australia as a whole. The table 
indicates significant decline in Applying and Reasoning since 2019 but not for Knowing.

82
KEY FINDINGS
YEAR 4 SCIENCE
	
z Australian students scored an average of 550 points on the TIMSS Year 4 science scale.
	
z Australia outperformed 49 countries, including the United States, Ireland, Canada and New Zealand, 
but was outperformed by 4 countries: Singapore, the top-performing country, Korea, Chinese Taipei and 
Türkiye.
	
z 83% of Australian students achieved the National Proficient Standard (NPS) in TIMSS Year 4 science. 
In Singapore, 93% reached this level.
	
z 17% of Australian Year 4 students were very high performers and 5% were very low performers. In 
comparison, 44% of students in Singapore were very high performers and only 2% were very low 
performers.
	
z Australia’s Year 4 science achievement in TIMSS 2023 was 28 points higher than in 1995 and 17 points 
higher than in 2019.
	
z Since 1995, the proportion of students who achieved the NPS has increased from 72% to 83%. The 
percentage of very high performers has increased from 13% to 17% and the percentage of very low 
performers has decreased from 11% to 5%.
	
z Year 4 students in the Northern Territory, New South Wales and Victoria improved in science 
achievement from 2019 by 46, 22 and 18 points, respectively. For the Northern Territory, this was 
accompanied by a significant increase in the proportion of students who achieved the NPS and a 
significant decrease in the percentage of very low performers.
	
z Year 4 students in Queensland, New South Wales, Victoria and South Australia improved in science 
achievement since 1995 by over 20 points. Most of these jurisdictions also experienced significant 
positive changes in the proportions who achieved the NPS and the percentages of very high and very 
low performers.
	
z Male students outperformed female students by 10 points, and also outperformed female students in 
terms of the percentage of very high performers.
	
z 89% of students who had many books in the home and 88% of students who had an average number 
of books in the home achieved the NPS, compared to 73% of those who reported having a few books in 
the home.
	
z First Nations students scored an average of 499 points on the TIMSS Year 4 science scale and 
63% achieved the NPS. The average science score of students with a First Nations background has 
improved by 50 points since 1995, narrowing the gap with non-First Nations students from 76 points to 
59 points in 2023.
	
z There were no significant differences in Year 4 science achievement between students who spoke a 
language other than English at home and students who spoke English at home.
	
z Students who attended schools in major cities scored 16 points higher than students who attended 
schools in regional areas.
	
z Australian Year 4 students performed relatively lower in the content domain of Physical science. The 
scores for Life science and Earth science were not significantly different to the overall science score.
	
z There were no relative differences between the overall science score and the cognitive domains for 
Year 4 science.
	
z Australian Year 4 students scored 550 points on the Environmental knowledge scale, similar to the 
overall science score.
	
z 53% of Australian Year 4 students said that they very strongly value environmental preservation. These 
students scored 31 points higher on Environmental knowledge than the 6% of students who only 
somewhat value environmental preservation.

83
83
KEY FINDINGS
YEAR 8 SCIENCE
	
z Australian students scored an average of 520 points on the TIMSS Year 8 science scale.
	
z Australia outperformed 27 countries, including Norway and the United Arab Emirates, but was 
outperformed by 7 countries, including England and Finland, as well as Singapore, the top-
performing country.
	
z 70% of Australian students achieved the National Proficient Standard (NPS) in TIMSS Year 8 
science. Most students (over 80%) in the highest performing countries of Singapore, Chinese 
Taipei, Japan and Korea reached this level.
	
z 13% of Australian Year 8 students were very high performers and 11% were very low performers. 
In comparison, 47% of students in Singapore were very high performers and only 3% were very 
low performers.
	
z Australia’s Year 8 mean score in science achievement has not changed significantly since either 
2019 or 1995.
	
z Since 1995, there has been no change in the proportion of students who achieved the NPS, but 
there was a decline of 4 percentage points since 2019.
	
z There was very little significant change in performance over time across the jurisdictions. 
Victoria improved in performance (by 23 points) since 1995, while New South Wales declined in 
performance since 2019 (by 25 points).
	
z Male students outperformed female students by 13 points, and also outperformed female 
students in terms of the percentage of very high performers.
	
z Students who had many educational resources in the home scored 56 points higher than students 
who had some educational resources in the home, and 121 points higher than those who reported 
having few educational resources in the home.
	
z First Nations background students scored an average of 451 points on the TIMSS Year 8 science 
scale and 44% achieved the NPS.
	
z Students who spoke English at home scored 18 points higher than those who spoke a language 
other than English at home. The percentage of students who spoke English at home who were 
very low performers was significantly lower than students who spoke a language other than 
English at home (by 6 percentage points).
	
z 72% of students who attended schools in major cities, compared to 64% who attended schools 
in regional areas, achieved the NPS.
	
z Australian Year 8 students performed relatively higher in the content domain of Physics and 
Earth science, but relatively lower in Biology and Chemistry.
	
z Australian Year 8 students performed relatively higher in the cognitive domain of Reasoning, but 
relatively lower in Knowing. The score for Applying was not significantly different to the overall 
science score.
	
z Australian Year 8 students scored 519 on the Environmental knowledge scale, similar to the 
overall science score.
	
z 38% of Australian Year 8 students said that they very strongly value environmental preservation. 
These students scored 43 points higher on Environmental knowledge than the 17% of students 
who only somewhat value environmental preservation.

84
CHAPTER 5
THE TIMSS 2023 SCIENCE 
FRAMEWORK AND BENCHMARKS
Possessing scientific knowledge gives children the ability to understand the world around them and to 
engage with the processes and purposes of scientific inquiry. According to Centurino and Kelly (2021):
Innovations in technology and science constantly shape and reshape our daily life experiences: the 
availability of clean water; the food we eat; the quality of the air we breathe; the vaccines, medicines, and 
medical diagnostic tools available; the ways we communicate; the modes of transportation we use; and 
more. Children in primary and lower-secondary grades today have lived all of their lives in a science and 
technology-enhanced world. Perhaps more than any previous generation, they have boundless opportunities 
to engage in activities and experiences and with information that can satisfy their natural curiosity about the 
world and their place in it (p.19).
The TIMSS 2023 science framework (2021) is organised around 2 dimensions – a content dimension, which 
specifies the subject matter to be assessed within science (for example, Life science or Chemistry) and a 
cognitive dimension, which specifies the thinking processes and sets of behaviours expected of students as 
they engage with the science content.
TIMSS science also assesses scientific practices. These include skills from daily life and school studies that 
students use in a systematic way to conduct scientific inquiry and investigation and are fundamental to all 
science disciplines. These are the scientific practices involved in scientific inquiry and include:
	
z asking questions based on observations and theories
	
z designing investigations and generating evidence
	
z working with data
	
z answering research questions
	
z making arguments from evidence.
TIMSS assesses the science practices primarily with the science problem-solving and inquiry (PSI) tasks, in 
which students conduct extended investigations and inquiries, thus engaging in one or more of the science 
practices. However, regular items in TIMSS can also incorporate one or more of the science practices. For 
more information about the science practices, please refer to the TIMSS 2023 science framework (2021).

Chapter 5 The TIMSS 2023 science framework and benchmarks
85
5.1	
Year 4 and Year 8 science content and cognitive domains
The TIMSS 2023 science framework (2021) defines 3 content domains for Year 4 students (Life science, 
Physical science; Earth science) and 4 content domains (Biology; Chemistry; Physics; Earth science) for Year 8 
students.
Each of these content domains has several topic areas. For example, the domain Life science includes 
characteristics and life processes of organisms; life cycles, reproduction and heredity; organisms, 
environment and their interactions; ecosystems; and human health. These topic areas are shown in 
Table 5.1.
For a detailed description of each of the content domains in science, please refer to the TIMSS 2023 science 
framework (2021).

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
To respond correctly to TIMSS test items, students need to be familiar with the science content of the 
items. Just as importantly, however, items are designed to elicit the use of particular cognitive skills. The 
TIMSS 2023 assessment framework presents detailed descriptions of the skills and abilities that make 
up the cognitive domains and that are assessed in conjunction with the content. The student behaviours 
encompassed by the cognitive dimension have been classified into 3 domains within the assessment 
framework.
The 3 domains can be described as follows:
	
z Knowing  – the student’s ability to recall, recognise, describe, and provide examples of facts, concepts, 
and procedures that are necessary for a solid foundation in science
	
z Applying  – using knowledge to compare, contrast, and classify groups of objects or materials; relating 
knowledge of a science concept to a specific context; generating explanations; and solving practical 
problems
	
z Reasoning – using evidence and science understanding to analyse, synthesise, and generalise, often in 
unfamiliar situations and complex contexts.
Table 5.2 shows the percentage of time devoted to each cognitive domain at Year 4 and Year 8. These 3 
cognitive domains are used for both Year 4 and Year 8, but the balance of testing time differs in each year 
level, reflecting the difference in age and experience of the students tested.
TABLE 5.2 TIMSS Year 4 and Year 8 science cognitive domains and percentage of assessment for each domain

5.2	
Year 4 and Year 8 science international benchmarks
The TIMSS science achievement scales summarise Year 4 and Year 8 students’ performance when 
interacting with a variety of scientific tasks and questions (please see the Reader’s guide for more 
information about the achievement scales). Students’ achievement is based on their responses to test 
questions designed to assess a range of content areas. When comparing groups of students across and 
within countries, summary statistics such as the average, or mean, scale score are often used.
This score, however, does not provide detailed information as to what types of scientific tasks the students 
were able to undertake successfully. Instead, TIMSS uses international benchmarks to provide descriptions 
of achievement on the scales in relation to performance on the questions asked.
Internationally, it was decided that performance should be measured at 4 levels. These 4 levels summarise 
the achievement reached by:
	
z the Advanced international benchmark, which was set at 625 points
	
z the High international benchmark, which was set at 550 points
	
z the Intermediate international benchmark, which was set at 475 points
	
z the Low international benchmark, which was set at 400 points.
The descriptions of the levels are cumulative, so that a student who reached the High benchmark typically 
demonstrated the knowledge and skills for both the Intermediate and the Low benchmarks. Table 5.3 
provides a summary of the TIMSS 2023 Year 4 and Year 8 science benchmarks.

Chapter 5 The TIMSS 2023 science framework and benchmarks

5.2.1	
The National Proficient Standard
In Australia, the Intermediate international benchmark has been designated as the National Proficient 
Standard. This level represents ‘a challenging but reasonable’ level of performance where students need to 
demonstrate more than the minimal skills expected (ACARA, 2020).
At Year 4, students at the Intermediate benchmark in science show and apply knowledge of some scientific 
concepts.
For example, Figure 5.1 shows an item from the Physical science domain that Year 4 students performing at 
the Intermediate benchmark could answer correctly. This item demonstrates the cognitive skill of Knowing. 
This item required students to identify which of 6 objects can be picked up using a magnet.
At Year 8, students at the Intermediate benchmark can apply understanding of some concepts from biology, 
chemistry, physics, and Earth science, and they engage in some scientific practices.
For example, Figure 5.2 shows an item from the content domain Biology, demonstrating the cognitive skill of 
Reasoning. This item required students to justify an advantage of hollow bones for birds.
Further information about the types of science skills and strategies demonstrated by Year 4 and Year 8 
students who performed at each of the international benchmarks, along with examples of the types of 
responses provided by students at each of the benchmarks, is provided in the TIMSS 2023 international 
results in mathematics and science (von Davier, et al., forthcoming 2024).

CHAPTER 6
AUSTRALIA’S YEAR 4 
SCIENCE RESULTS
In this report, the focus is on differences that are statistically significant (in other words, are unlikely 
to have occurred by chance). Where the commentary states that there was a difference between sets 
of numbers, whether these were scores, percentages or percentage point differences, it means that 
the difference satisfied this condition. Where the commentary states that there was no difference, or 
where no comment is made regarding a possible comparison, it indicates that the difference was not 
statistically significant.
Due to large standard errors for some results, differences that appear numerically larger may not be 
statistically significant. For more information about standard errors and statistical significance, please 
refer to the Reader’s Guide.
6.1	
Year 4 science results within the international context
This section reports the TIMSS 2023 science results as average scores and distributions on the TIMSS 
Year 4 science scale (please see the Reader’s guide for more information about the achievement scales).
Australian Year 4 students achieved a mean score of 550 points in science. This performance was lower 
than students in 4 countries (Singapore, Korea, Chinese Taipei and Türkiye); not significantly different to 
students in 4 countries (England, Japan, Poland and Hong Kong) and higher than students in 49 countries 
(including the United States, Ireland, Canada and New Zealand).
Figure 6.1 provides the mean scores achieved by students in each country, the standard error and the 
confidence interval of each mean and the standard deviation (an indication in the variability of individual 
scores around the mean). Countries are positioned from highest to lowest level of achievement; however, 
this is not a simple ranking, as the differences between countries may not be statistically significant. 
The shading indicates whether the score for a given country was significantly different to Australia’s. To 
determine whether or not differences between other countries were statistically significant, please refer to 
the TIMSS 2023 international results in mathematics and science (von Davier, et al., forthcoming 2024).
Singapore was the highest performing country of TIMSS 2023 with 607 points, which was significantly higher 
than all other countries. Korea was the next highest with 583 points, followed by Chinese Taipei (573) and 
Türkiye (570). The mean score for these high performing countries exceeded the High benchmark of 550 
points. The mean scores for England (556), Japan (555), Poland (550) and Australia (550) also just reached 
the High benchmark of 550 points.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
6.1.1	
Performance at the international benchmarks
In addition to analysing performance according to mean scores, the international benchmarks give further 
beneficial insight into student achievement.
Figure 6.2 shows the percentages of students in each country at each of the international benchmarks. The 
countries are ordered by the percentages of students who reached the Intermediate international benchmark, 
which is the National Proficient Standard (NPS) set for TIMSS science in Australia (please see the Reader’s 
guide for more information about the NPS).
Students who attained the National Proficient Standard
In TIMSS 2023, 83% of Australian students attained the NPS in Year 4 science, compared to the international 
median of 70%.
In Singapore and Korea, 93% of students achieved at least the Intermediate international benchmark. There 
was similar achievement in Chinese Taipei (91%) and Japan (88%).
Very high performers
In Australia, 17% of Year 4 students were very high performers in science, compared to the international 
median of 7%.
The countries with the highest average science achievement were also the countries with the largest 
percentages of students who reached the Advanced benchmark. In Singapore, 44% of Year 4 students were 
very high performers; the next highest was Korea with 28%. In Türkiye and Chinese Taipei, the proportions 
were 26% and 23%, respectively.
Australia was one of only 20 countries that had 10% or more of very high performing students. Five countries 
(Bosnia & Herzegovina, Uzbekistan, Armenia, Azerbaijan and Kosovo) had fewer than 1% of students who 
attained the Advanced benchmark.
Very low performers
In Australia, 5% of Year 4 students were very low performers in science, compared to the international 
median of 10%.
In Chinese Taipei, only 1% of students did not achieve the Low international benchmark, while in Singapore, 
Korea and Japan, the proportions were 2%.
The proportion of very low performing students was less than 10% in most countries. However, in 17 
countries, the proportion was more than 20% and in 3 countries (Morocco, Kuwait and South Africa), it was 
more than 50%.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
6.1.2	
Trends in Year 4 science achievement
Looking at the overall trends in Year 4 science achievement between 2019 and 2023, just under half of the 
participating countries had no significant change in score. Almost equal numbers of countries had increases 
and decreases in average science achievement. However, for countries that participated in the 1995 and 
2023 cycles, most had increases and very small numbers had either a decrease or no change in average 
science achievement.
Since 2019:
	
z 15 out of 49 countries had increases in average science achievement.
	
z 13 out of 49 countries had decreases in average science achievement.
	
z Saudi Arabia had the highest increase in achievement (by 25 points) followed by Qatar (by 23 points) then 
the United Arab Emirates (by 22 points).
	
z Kazakhstan had the largest decrease in achievement (by 28 points).
	
z Australia’s average science achievement increased by 17 points.
Since 1995:
	
z 11 out of 16 countries had increases in average science achievement.
	
z 2 out of 16 countries had decreases in average science achievement.
	
z Singapore had the greatest increase (by 84 points), followed by Slovenia (by 62 points) and Portugal (59 
points).
	
z Australia’s average science achievement increased by 28 points.
Patterns of achievement
Figure 6.3 graphs trends in Year 4 science achievement for selected countries with comparable data from 
TIMSS 1995 to 2023. The comparison countries are the United States, England, New Zealand, Singapore and 
Hungary, which have participated in all cycles since 1995. The patterns of achievement through the 8 cycles 
of TIMSS have varied considerably across countries.6
Between 1995 and 2023, the following key trends are noted:
	
z Singapore recorded the greatest and largest improvement between 1995 and 2023 (by 84 points).
	
z England has improved and declined, and then improved again, with an overall improvement of 28 points 
since 1995.
	
z Likewise, New Zealand has followed a similar pattern of improvement, although their average scale score 
in 2023 was not significantly different to 1995.
	
z Hungary showed significant improvement from 1995 to 2015 (34 points), but then a decline of 18 points 
between 2015 and 2023.
	
z The United States has experienced very little variation over the years; the greatest significant change was 
a decrease of 13 points since 2015, but their average scale score in 2023 was not significantly different to 
2019.
	
z Australia’s pattern of science achievement is one of slight improvement, decline and then continued 
improvement through to 2023, with an average scale score 28 points higher than TIMSS 1995. Australia’s 
average scale score for TIMSS 2023 is significantly higher than TIMSS 2019 (by 17 points) and all other 
cycles.
Figure 6.4 shows the distribution of science achievement for Australian Year 4 students across the cycles. 
Interestingly, the spread of science achievement has decreased since 1995. This has been because of a 
reduction in very low performing students.
6	
There was no Year 4 data collection in 1999, so only 7 cycles of data are available for Year 4.

FIGURE 6.4 Trends in Year 4 science achievement score and distribution, 1995–2023, Australia
Relative achievement
Table 6.1 displays each country’s position relative to Australia’s in each TIMSS cycle (please see Appendix B 
for the mean scores and standard errors by cycle for each country).
Australia’s relative position in TIMSS 2023 has improved since 2019. Japan outperformed Australia in 2019, 
but their performance was not significantly different to Australia’s in 2023.
In contrast, Lithuania, Sweden, Ireland, the United States, Czech Republic and Hungary, all performed at a 
similar level to Australia in 2019 but scored significantly lower than Australia in 2023. Finland, Norway and 
Latvia scored higher than Australia in 2019 but lower in 2023.
In terms of trends since 1995, Singapore’s performance in Year 4 science was not statistically different to 
Australia’s in 1995 but has improved over the past 28 years and achieved significantly higher than Australia in 
2023. Hong Kong scored significantly lower than Australia in TIMSS 1995 but was not statistically different to 
Australia in 2023. Japan scored significantly higher than Australia in 1995 but was not significantly different 
in 2023. The United States and Czech Republic scored significantly higher than Australia in TIMSS 1995, but 
both fell significantly lower than Australia in 2023. Ireland and the Netherlands were not statistically different 
to Australia in TIMSS 1995 but scored significantly lower than Australia in 2023.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
Very high and very low performers
Figure 6.5 shows the trends in the percentages of very high and very low performing students between 
TIMSS 1995 and 2023, by country.
Between 1995 and 2023, 10 of the 16 countries significantly increased the percentages of students who 
achieved the Advanced benchmark. And only 3 countries (the United States, the Czech Republic and the 
Netherlands) experienced a significant decrease in the percentages of students who achieved the Advanced 
benchmark. The proportion of very high performers increased by 4 percentage points in Australia.
For the majority of countries (9 out of 16) that participated in both TIMSS 1995 and 2023, the percentages 
of students who achieved the Low benchmark in Year 4 science significantly decreased. Only in one country 
(the Netherlands) did the proportion of very low performers increase. The proportion of very low performers 
decreased by 6 percentage points in Australia.

Students who attained the National Proficient Standard
Figure 6.6 shows the trends in the percentages of Australian Year 4 students who achieved at each of the 
international benchmarks and the proportions of students who attained the NPS. The proportion of students 
who attained the NPS has increased significantly since 1995.
The 1995 to 2023 trends show a significant decrease in the percentage of students at and below the Low 
international benchmark and a significant increase in the percentage of students at the High and Advanced 
international benchmarks. The change at the Low and Advanced benchmarks is also significant between 
2019 and 2023.

6.2	
Year 4 science results within the national context
Figure 6.7 presents the mean and distribution of Year 4 science performance for each of the Australian 
jurisdictions for TIMSS 2023. Singapore is included for comparison. The jurisdictions are shown in order 
from highest to lowest mean scores.
Read Figure 6.7 in conjunction with Table 6.2, which presents multiple comparisons of science performance 
between jurisdictions and indicates whether a jurisdiction’s performance does, or does not, differ 
significantly from another’s. The mean scores ranged from 563 points in the Australian Capital Territory to 
530 points in Tasmania.
The average science score of students in the Australian Capital Territory was not significantly different to the 
New South Wales or Victoria. Students in the Australian Capital Territory and New South Wales performed 
significantly higher than students in Queensland, South Australia, Western Australia, Northern Territory and 
Tasmania. Students in Victoria performed significantly higher than students in the Northern Territory and 
Tasmania.
Western Australia had the largest standard deviation in student performance (87 points), while Australian 
Capital Territory had the smallest standard deviation in performance (74 points).

Chapter 6 Australia’s Year 4 science results
101
The Australian Capital Territory had the highest proportions of students who attained the NPS (88%) and the 
Northern Territory had the lowest (75%). The proportion of students who attained the NPS in the Australian 
Capital Territory was significantly higher than all other jurisdictions except Victoria and New South Wales; 
Victoria was significantly higher than all other jurisdictions except the Australian Capital Territory, New South 
Wales, and South Australia; and New South Wales was significantly higher than Tasmania and the Northern 
Territory.
The Australian Capital Territory had the highest percentage of very high performers (21%). Tasmania had the 
lowest percentage of high performers (11%). For comparison, 44% of students in Singapore achieved the 
Advanced benchmark.
The Australian Capital Territory had the lowest percentage of very low performers, only 3% of students did 
not reach the Low benchmark (below Low). Western Australia, Tasmania, and the Northern Territory had 7% 
of students who were very low performers. In Singapore, however, only 2% of students fell below the Low 
benchmark.
Most differences in the percentages of very high and very low performing students between the jurisdictions 
were not significant. However, the percentage of very high performers was significantly higher in the 
Australian Capital Territory and New South Wales than in Tasmania. The percentage of very low performers 
was significantly lower in the Australian Capital Territory than in Queensland and Western Australia.
6.2.2	
Trends in Year 4 science achievement by jurisdiction
Figure 6.9 presents the trends in science achievement for each of the jurisdictions for each cycle of TIMSS 
since 1995 and an indication of the statistical significance of the difference between cycles (please see 
Appendix C for the mean scores and standard errors by cycle for each jurisdiction).

FIGURE 6.9 (continued) Trends in Year 4 science achievement within Australia, 1995–2023, by jurisdiction

104
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
The results show that there has been significant change in scores for some of the jurisdictions in science 
achievement at Year 4 over time. Northern Territory has significantly improved since 2019, with an increase 
of 46 points. New South Wales and Victoria also significantly improved since 2019 by 22 points and 18 
points, respectively. Queensland, New South Wales, Victoria and South Australia all improved significantly 
since 1995 (by 39, 34, 24 and 22 points respectively).
Figure 6.10 shows the proportions of students who attained the NPS, as well as the percentages of very high 
and very low performers in Year 4 science, for each Australian jurisdiction for all TIMSS cycles from 1995 
through to 2023.
The Northern Territory and New South Wales showed significant improvement in the proportions of students 
who attained the NPS since 2019 (by 20 and 6 percentage points, respectively). New South Wales, Victoria, 
Queensland and South Australia improved significantly since 1995.
Since 1995, there have been significant changes in the percentages of high performers in New South Wales 
and Queensland. Since 2019, the percentages of very high achievers increased significantly in New South 
Wales, Victoria and Western Australia.
Since 1995, the percentages of very low achievers decreased significantly in New South Wales, Victoria, 
Queensland, South Australia and the Northern Territory. Since 2019, the percentage of very low performers 
decreased significantly in the Northern Territory (by 13 percentage points).
