TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
6.3	
Year 4 science results for different demographic groups
6.3.1	
Results by gender
Figure 6.11 shows international Year 4 science achievement for male and female students. The average 
achievement is shown separately for females and males, as well as the differences between the averages. 
Differences are shown by a bar indicating the size and direction (in favour of males or females) and whether 
the difference was statistically significant (indicated by a darkened bar). Countries are presented in order of 
the highest to lowest difference between females and males in average achievement.
Overall, there was little difference in achievement between females and males. For 26 countries, there were 
no significant gender differences in Year 4 science achievement. Of the remaining countries, including 
Australia, 20 had significant differences that favoured male students, and 12 had significant differences 
favouring females.
Some of these differences were quite large, particularly those in favour of females. Five countries (South 
Africa, Bahrain, Saudi Arabia, Jordan and Kuwait) had a difference of over 20 points in favour of females.
Figure 6.12 shows the mean score and distribution of science achievement for male and female Year 4 
students in Australia, including the standard deviation of the mean. It shows that there was a significant 
difference in the mean score between male and female students, with males performing higher by 10 points. 
However, the variability in scores was greater for Year 4 males (SD = 84) than for Year 4 females (SD = 78). 
This is seen in the distribution of scores where the highest performing male students achieved at a slightly 
higher level than the highest performing female students (but there was little difference at the lower end of 
the distribution).

FIGURE 6.12 Mean scores and distribution of Year 4 science achievement within Australia, by gender
Performance at the international benchmarks by gender
Figure 6.13 presents the percentages of female and male Year 4 students at each of the international 
benchmarks for science as well as the proportions of students who attained the NPS.
The difference in the proportions of female and male students who attained the NPS was not significant.
However, as was seen in the distributions of scores, there were slightly more male than female students 
at the higher end of the scale; significantly more male students achieved the Advanced benchmark (by 6 
percentage points).
There were no significant differences in the percentages of very low performers.

FIGURE 6.13 Percentages of Australian students at the international benchmarks for Year 4 science and proportions 
of students who attained the National Proficient Standard, by gender
Trends in science achievement by gender
Figure 6.14 shows the trends in the science achievement of Year 4 male and female students in Australia 
from 1995 to 2023. This figure shows that TIMSS 2023 is the first TIMSS cycle to show a significant 
difference between males and females in Year 4 science in Australia. However, since 1995, both males and 
females have increased their average scales score; the average score for males increased by a significant 31 
points and females by a significant 26 points.

FIGURE 6.14 Trends in Year 4 science achievement within Australia, 1995–2023, by gender
Gender difference in science achievement by jurisdiction
Figure 6.15 shows the gender differences in Year 4 science by jurisdiction. Queensland had the only 
significant gender difference of 11 points, in favour of male students.
Figure 6.16 shows the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks and the proportions 
of students who attained the NPS in each jurisdiction, by gender.
There were no significant differences in the proportions of male and female students who achieved the NPS 
in any jurisdiction.
In both New South Wales and Victoria, there were significant differences in the percentages of male and 
female students who achieved the Advanced benchmark; a higher proportion of males achieved the 
Advanced benchmark than females.
There were no significant differences in the percentages of very low performing male and female students in 
any jurisdiction.

6.3.2	
Results by books in the home
This section presents Year 4 Australian students’ science achievement according to the number of books in 
the home. For more information about this variable, please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Figure 6.17 provides the percentages of students in each category and their average achievement score. 
Fifty-four per cent of Australian Year 4 students reported having an average number of books and 16% 
reported having many books at home. The students who had many books in the home also had the highest 
levels of achievement, and scored 15 points on average higher than students who had an average number of 
books in the home, and 59 points higher than those who had a few books in the home. The students who had 
an average number of books in the home scored 44 points on average higher than students who had a few 
books in the home. All differences in scale scores were significant.
Figure 6.17 also shows the distribution of scores in science achievement of Year 4 students for each 
category of books at home. As is indicated by the standard deviation, those who reported having many 
books in the home had the widest range of scores (SD = 84), while the group that reported an average 
number of books at home had the narrowest (SD = 74).
Figure 6.18 presents the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks for science as well 
as the proportions of students who attained the NPS, by the number of books in the home.
Eighty-nine per cent of students who reported having many books in the home and 88% of students with an 
average number of books in the home attained the NPS, significantly higher than the 73% of those with only a 
few books in the home.
In terms of very high performing students, 30% of students who reported having many books in the home 
achieved the Advanced benchmark. Only 19% of students in the average number of books category and just 
8% in the a few books category attained this level of achievement. These differences were all significant.
At the other end of the scale, 4% of students who reported having many books in the home and 3% with 
an average number of books in the home did not achieve even the Low international benchmark, this was 
significantly lower than the 9% of students who reported having a few books in the home.

6.3.3	
Results by First Nations background
‘Supporting Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander learners to reach their full potential’ is one of the areas for 
action identified in the Alice Springs (Mparntwe) education declaration (Education Council, 2019, p.16). As 
such, educational outcomes for young First Nations Australians are a key area of interest for the education 
community. This section presents Australian students’ science achievement according to whether students 
identified as being a First Nations Australian. For more information about this variable, please refer to the 
Reader’s guide.
Ten per cent of the TIMSS sample at Year 4 identified as being a First Nations Australian. As shown in Figure 
6.19, First Nations students attained an average score in science of 499 points. This figure also presents the 
distribution of Year 4 achievement scores. As is indicated by the standard deviation, the spread of scores for 
First Nations students is slightly larger (SD = 85) than the spread of scores for non-First Nations students 
(SD = 78).

Figure 6.21 shows the trends in achievement of First Nations students from 1995 to 2023. The average

the gap with non-First Nations students from 76 points to 59 points in 2023.

6.3.4	
Results by language spoken at home
The frequency with which English is spoken at home is a factor that has been associated with Year 4 science 
achievement in past cycles of TIMSS. Students who come from homes in which English is not spoken 
frequently may have had less exposure to the language of instruction and test, which could place them 
at a disadvantage. However, this variable can be difficult to interpret, as this is a very diverse group. While 
it includes those migrants to Australia who have limited English language skills at home, there is also a 
substantial proportion of students in this group who are economic immigrants to Australia, and, while English 
may not be the main language at home, this is no barrier to their understanding of science, in particular.
This section presents Year 4 science achievement according to whether a language other than English was 
spoken as the main language at home. For more information about this variable, please refer to the Reader’s 
guide.
Figure 6.22 shows that 21% of students in the TIMSS Year 4 sample indicated that they did not speak English 
at home always or almost always. Students who spoke mainly English at home and students who spoke a 
language other than English at home performed at a similar level (552 and 546 points, respectively).
Figure 6.22 also shows the distribution of scores for students by their language background. As is indicated 
by the standard deviation, the spread of scores for students who spoke a language other than English at 
home (SD = 86) was larger than that of students with an English-speaking background (SD = 79).

Figure 6.23 presents the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks for science as well 
as the proportions of students who attained the NPS by language background.
There were no significant differences in the proportions of students who attained the NPS nor in the 
percentages achieved at each of the benchmarks according to language background.

6.3.5	
Results by geographic location of the school
Past cycles of TIMSS have found that students who attended schools in remote or regional areas of 
Australia are often at an educational disadvantage compared to students who attended schools in major 
cities. This section presents Australian students’ science achievement according to the geographic location 
of the school, reported using the Australian Bureau of Statistics Australian statistical geography standard 
remoteness structure (ABS, 2011). For more information about this variable, please refer to the Reader’s 
guide.
Figure 6.24 shows the Year 4 science performance of students from schools according to the geographic 
location of the school. Students who attended schools in remote areas made up only 1% of the Year 4 TIMSS 
sample, while those who attended schools in major cities made up 74% of the sample. Students in schools in 
major cities scored, on average, 16 points higher than students who attended schools in regional areas, and 
13 points, on average, higher than students in schools in remote areas. Only the difference between schools 
in major cities and regional areas was significant (due to the large standard error for the remote areas 
category).
Figure 6.24 also provides the spread of scores for science achievement by geographic location of school. As 
is indicated by the standard deviation, the spread of scores for students who attended schools in major cities 
(80 points) and regional areas (SD = 81) was similar. However, students who attended schools in remote 
areas had a greater spread than the other 2 groups (SD = 86).

Figure 6.25 shows the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks and the proportions

6.4	
Year 4 science results in the TIMSS content and cognitive domains
As noted in Chapter 5, the TIMSS science assessment can be described in terms of content and cognitive 
domains. The content domains outline the subject matter to be assessed and include Life science, Physical 
science and Earth science at Year 4. The cognitive domains detail the thinking processes that students will 
need to use. The cognitive domains are Knowing, Applying and Reasoning. Each item is associated with a 
single content domain and a single cognitive domain. This allows student performance to be described in 
terms of achievement in each of the domains.
To allow comparisons of student achievement across the domains, the content and cognitive achievement 
scales at each year level were constructed to have the same average level of difficulty. The following tables 
present the average achievement in each of the Year 4 science content and cognitive domains for Australia 
as a whole, for each of the Australian jurisdictions, for males and females, and by First Nations background. 
These tables also show trends for Australia in the content and cognitive domains since 2007.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
6.4.1	
Science content domains: Life science, Physical science, and 
Earth science
Table 6.3 provides the Year 4 achievement scores in the science content domains for Australia, the 
jurisdictions, by gender and by First Nations background.
Australian Year 4 students’ relative performance across the domains was not statistically significant, except 
for Physical science where they performed relatively less well.
However, at the jurisdiction level, due to large standard errors, there were no statistically significant 
differences, despite similar patterns of relative performance across the domains.
The relatively poorer performance for Physical science was true for female students and non-First Nations 
students. However, female students showed relatively stronger performance for Life science.
Male students had significantly higher average achievement in Earth science and Physical science compared 
to female students.
Table 6.4 shows the trends in achievement for the content domains for Australia as a whole. Australian 
Year 4 performance in all 3 content domains was significantly higher in 2023 than for all other cycles. The 
improvement since 2019 was 15 points in Life science, 19 points in Physical science and 22 points in Earth 
science.

6.4.2	
Science cognitive domains: Knowing, Applying, and Reasoning
Table 6.5 presents the Year 4 achievement scores in the science cognitive domains for Australia, the 
jurisdictions, by gender and by First Nations background.
Australian Year 4 students’ relative performance was similar across the cognitive domains, with no

achievement in Knowing and Applying, than female students.

indicates significant improvement in all 3 domains Knowing, Applying and Reasoning since 2019 and all other 
cycles since 2007.

Chapter 6 Australia’s Year 4 science results
121
6.5	
Environmental awareness
TIMSS 2023 includes an expanded focus on environmental awareness, assessing both knowledge and 
attitudes. By assessing both knowledge and attitudes, TIMSS 2023 provides a more comprehensive 
understanding of students’ environmental literacy, and thus their ability to make informed decisions about 
sustainability and actively participating in addressing environmental challenges.
This section covers Year 4 students’ achievement on the Environmental knowledge subscale, as well as their 
attitudes towards the preservation of the environment.
6.5.1	
Environmental knowledge: Results within the international context
To understand what students around the world know about environmental issues, the Environmental 
knowledge subscale was constructed based on the achievement items covering environmental science in the 
TIMSS 2023 assessment. At Year 4, the Environmental knowledge subscale included 44 items drawn from 
the Life and Earth science subdomains. The range of items addressed a variety of environmental topics, from 
both local issues such as water pollution to global issues such as climate change and its impact.
Australian Year 4 students achieved a mean score of 550 points in Environmental knowledge. This 
performance was lower than students in 3 countries (Singapore, Korea and Chinese Taipei); not significantly 
different to students in 5 countries (Poland, England, Türkiye, Hong Kong and Finland) and higher than 
students in 48 countries (including Ireland, United States, Sweden, Canada, New Zealand and the United Arab 
Emirates).
Figure 6.26 provides the mean scores achieved by students in each country, the standard error and 
confidence interval of each mean and the standard deviation (an indication in the variability of individual 
scores around the mean). Countries are positioned from highest to lowest level of achievement; however, 
this is not a simple ranking, as the differences between countries may not be statistically significant. 
The shading indicates whether the score for a given country was significantly different to Australia’s. To 
determine whether differences between other countries were statistically significant, please refer to the 
TIMSS 2023 international results in mathematics and science (von Davier et al., 2024).
Australia’s mean score of 550 points in Environmental knowledge was no different to the overall score in 
science (550 points). However, many countries did show a difference in achievement in Environmental 
knowledge compared to their overall score in science. Nineteen of the countries had relative achievement 
in Environmental knowledge that was lower than their overall score in science (including Singapore, Korea, 
Chinese Taipei and Japan); while 13 countries had relative achievement in Environmental knowledge that was 
higher than their overall score in science (including Poland, Ireland, United States and Hungary).

6.5.2	
Environmental knowledge: Results within the national context
Figure 6.27 presents the mean and distribution of Year 4 students’ achievement in Environmental knowledge 
performance for each of the Australian jurisdictions and for males and females and by First Nations 
background for TIMSS 2023. Figure 6.27 is supported by Table 6.7, which presents multiple comparisons 
of achievement in Environmental knowledge between the jurisdictions and indicates whether a jurisdiction’s 
performance does, or does not, differ significantly from another’s.
The mean scores in Environmental knowledge for each of the Australian jurisdictions and for males and 
female students and by First Nations background did not differ significantly from the mean score on overall 
science. Therefore, the pattern of achievement was similar for Environmental knowledge as for overall 
science.
Comparing performance across the jurisdictions, the Australian Capital Territory and New South Wales 
performed significantly higher than students in South Australia, Queensland, Western Australia, Tasmania 
and the Northern Territory. Students in Victoria also performed significantly higher than students in Western 
Australia, Tasmania and the Northern Territory.
In terms of performance by gender, male students scored on average 20 points higher than female students 
on Environmental knowledge.
In terms of performance by First Nations background, there was a 62 score point difference between First 
Nations and non-First Nations students on Environmental knowledge.

6.5.3	
Students value environmental preservation
TIMSS 2023 collected data on how strongly students value environmental preservation. Students were asked 
to indicate their level of agreement (agree a lot, agree a little, disagree a little or disagree a lot) with each of 
the following 5 statements:
	
z I care about the protection of plants and animals.
	
z It makes me sad when nature is destroyed by humans.
	
z I enjoy finding out what kinds of plants and animals live in my surrounding area.
	
z I enjoy being in nature (e.g. parks, beaches, the bush, deserts).
	
z Addressing climate change should be a high priority.
Responses to these statements were combined to create the Students value environmental preservation 
scale.
At Year 4, students who very strongly value environmental preservation had a score on the scale of at least 
9.8, which corresponds to their agreeing a lot with 3 of the statements and agreeing a little with the other 2, 
on average. Students who somewhat value environmental preservation had a score that was no higher than 
7.2, which corresponds to their disagreeing a little with 3 of the 5 statements and agreeing a little with the 
remaining 2. All other students strongly value environmental preservation.
Figure 6.28 shows the percentages of Year 4 students in each of the 3 categories of the Students value 
environmental preservation scale, and their associated scores in Environmental knowledge in each category.

FIGURE 6.28 The Students value environmental preservation scale and Year 4 student achievement in Environmental 
knowledge, Australia and the international average
Fifty-three per cent of Australian Year 4 students said that they very strongly value environmental 
preservation, and 41% reported that they strongly value environmental preservation, with only 6% indicating 
that they somewhat value environmental preservation. Internationally, 56% of students (on average across 
countries) said that they very strongly value environmental preservation, and 38% reported that they 
strongly value environmental preservation, with only 6% indicating that they somewhat value environmental 
preservation.
There was a positive relationship between valuing environmental preservation and average Environmental 
knowledge achievement. Australian Year 4 students who very strongly value environmental preservation 
scored 31 points higher on Environmental knowledge than students who only somewhat value environmental 
preservation, and those who strongly value environmental preservation scored 26 points higher on 
Environmental knowledge than students who only somewhat value environmental preservation.

CHAPTER 7
AUSTRALIA’S YEAR 8 
SCIENCE RESULTS
In this report, the focus is on differences that are statistically significant (in other words, are unlikely 
to have occurred by chance). Where the commentary states that there was a difference between sets 
of numbers, whether these were scores, percentages or percentage point differences, it means that 
the difference satisfied this condition. Where the commentary states that there was no difference, or 
where no comment is made regarding a possible comparison, it indicates that the difference was not 
statistically significant.
Due to large standard errors for some results, differences that appear numerically larger may not be 
statistically significant. For more information about standard errors and statistical significance, please 
refer to the Reader’s Guide.
7.1	
Year 8 science results within the international context
This section reports the TIMSS 2023 science results as average scores and distributions on the TIMSS Year 
8 science scale (please see the Reader’s guide for more information about the achievement scales).
Australian Year 8 students achieved a mean score of 520 points in science. This performance was lower 
than students in 7 countries (Singapore, Chinese Taipei, Japan, Korea, England, Finland and Türkiye); not 
significantly different to students in 7 countries (Hong Kong, Czech Republic, Ireland, Hungary, Sweden, 
Lithuania and the United States) and higher than students in 27 countries (including Portugal, Malta, Norway 
and the United Arab Emirates).
Figure 7.1 provides the mean scores achieved by students in each country, the standard error and confidence 
interval of each mean and the standard deviation (an indication in the variability of individual scores 
around the mean). Countries are positioned from highest to lowest level of achievement; however, this is 
not a simple ranking as the differences between countries may not be statistically significant. The shading 
indicates whether the score for a given country was significantly different to Australia. To determine whether 
or not differences between other countries were statistically significant, please refer to the TIMSS 2023 
international results in mathematics and science (von Davier, et al., forthcoming 2024).

FIGURE 7.1 Mean scores and distribution of Year 8 science achievement, by country
Singapore was the top-performing country in Year 8 science with a mean score of 606 points. The top 4 
highest performing countries were Singapore, Chinese Taipei (572), Japan (557) and Korea (545), all of 
whom performed significantly different to each other and significantly higher than the next group of countries 
(including England and Finland, which both had a mean score of 531). The mean score of Singapore, Chinese 
Taipei and Japan exceeded the High benchmark of 550 points.

7.1.1	
Performance at the international benchmarks
In addition to analysing performance according to mean scores, the international benchmarks give beneficial 
insight into student achievement.
Figure 7.2 shows the percentages of students in each country at each of the international benchmarks. The 
countries are ordered by the percentages of students who reached the Intermediate international benchmark, 
which is the National Proficient Standard (NPS) set for TIMSS science in Australia (please see the Reader’s 
guide for more information about the NPS).
Students who attained the National Proficient Standard
In TIMSS 2023, 70% of Australian students attained the NPS in Year 8 science, compared to the international 
median of 56%.
In Singapore, 89% of students achieved at least the Intermediate international benchmark. There was similar 
achievement in Chinese Taipei (85%), Japan (85%) and Korea (80%).
Very high performers
In Australia, 13% of Year 8 students were very high performers in science, compared to the international 
median of 6%.
The countries with the highest average science achievement were also the countries with the largest 
percentages of students who reached the Advanced benchmark. In Singapore, 47% of Year 8 students were 
very high performers, followed by Chinese Taipei with 30%. In Japan and Korea, 20% and 18% of students 
(respectively) were very high performers.
Australia was one of 17 countries that had 10% or more of very high performing students. Eleven countries 
(Malaysia, Saudi Arabia, Jordan, Brazil, South Africa, Georgia, Iran, Palestinian National Authority, Azerbaijan, 
Uzbekistan and Morocco) had 1% or fewer students who attained the Advanced benchmark.
Very low performers
In Australia, 11% of Year 8 students were very low performers in science, compared to the international 
median of 20%.
The top 3 countries (Singapore, Chinese Taipei, and Japan) all had fewer than 5% of students who performed 
below the Low international benchmark.
The proportion of very low performers was less than 20% in most countries. However, in 10 countries, the 
proportion was more than 40% and in 4 countries (Palestinian National Authority, Uzbekistan, South Africa 
and Morocco), it was more than 50%.

7.1.2	
Trends in Year 8 science achievement
Looking at the overall trends in Year 8 science achievement between 2019 and 2023, very few countries 
had increases; equal numbers of countries experienced a decrease or no change in average achievement. 
However, for countries that participated in the 1995 and 2023 cycles, about half had no significant change in 
score and there were almost equal numbers of countries with increases and decreases.
Since 2019:
	
z 3 out of 34 countries increased in average science achievement.
	
z 15 out of 34 countries decreased in average science achievement.
	
z Hong Kong had the highest increase in achievement (by 24 points) and Morocco had the largest decrease 
(by 67 points).
	
z Australia’s average science achievement decreased by 8 points, but this change is not statistically 
significant.
Since 1995:
	
z 5 out of 17 countries increased in average science achievement.
	
z 4 out of 17 countries decreased in average science achievement.
	
z Lithuania had the highest increase in achievement (by 56 points) and Iran had the largest decrease 
(by 44 points).
	
z Australia’s average science achievement increased by 6 points, but this change is not statistically 
significant.
Patterns of achievement
Figure 7.3 presents trends in Year 8 science achievement for selected countries with comparable data from 
TIMSS 1995 to 2023. The comparison countries are the United States, England, New Zealand, Singapore and 
Hungary, which have participated in all cycles since 1995. The patterns of achievement through the 8 cycles 
of TIMSS have varied considerably across countries.7
Between 1995 and 2023, the following key trends are noted:
	
z Singapore recorded the greatest improvement, with the largest single improvement between 2007 and 
2019, and held steady in 2023 (41 points).
	
z England and New Zealand both declined significantly between 2015 and 2019 (20 and 14 points, 
respectively). However, in 2023 England returned to a mean score similar to 1995.
	
z Hungary had a rise then a fall and then has held fairly steady since 2011, but at a level lower than their 
1995 mean score.
	
z The United States experienced an increase between 1995 and 2003, with a slight decline in 2007, rising to 
a peak in 2015, only to decrease back to the same mean score as 1995 in 2023.
Australia’s science achievement over the 28 years since 1995 appears to fluctuate around the 520 point 
mark. The largest change was a 16-point increase from 512 points in 2015 to 528 points in 2019. The 2015 
cycle had the lowest mean score of the 7 cycles and 2019 had the highest.
Figure 7.4 shows the distribution of science achievement for Australian Year 8 students across the cycles. 
Although the range of performance contracted in 2003, it has widened since then so that the distribution in 
2023 is similar to 1995.
7	
The Australian Year 8 sample that participated in 1999 was not comparable to that in other cycles, so no trend results are provided for Australia at Year 8 in 1999. Other 
countries may not have data for certain cycles if they did not participate in that cycle or had other inconsistencies that do not allow for trends.

134
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
Australia’s relative position in TIMSS 2023 has not changed much since 2019. England and Türkiye scored  
significantly lower than Australia in 2019 but scored significantly higher in 2023, and Hong Kong 
scored significantly lower than Australia in 2019 but performed at a similar level to Australia in 2023.
In terms of trends since 1995, Lithuania scored significantly lower than Australia in TIMSS 1995, but was not 
statistically different to Australia in 2023. Czech Republic, Hungary and Sweden scored significantly higher 
than Australia in 1995 but fell to be not statistically different to Australia in TIMSS 2023.
Very high and very low performers
Figure 7.5 shows the trends in the percentages of very high and very low performing students between 
TIMSS 1995 and 2023, by country.
In this period, 7 of the 17 countries significantly increased the percentages of students who achieved the 
Advanced benchmark. And in 3 countries (Sweden, the Czech Republic and Romania), the percentages 
of students who achieved the Advanced benchmark significantly decreased. The proportion of very high 
performers in Australia significantly increased by 3 points from 1995 to 2023.
In this period, only 3 of the 17 countries (Lithuania, Portugal and Cyprus) significantly decreased the 
percentages of students who achieved below the Low benchmark. And in 6 countries, the percentages 
of students who achieved below the Low benchmark significantly increased. The proportion of very low 
performers did not change in Australia.

Students who attained the National Proficient Standard
Figure 7.6 shows the trends in the percentages of Australian Year 8 students who achieved at the 
international benchmarks and the proportion of students who attained the NPS. Since 2019, this proportion 
decreased significantly by 4 percentage points but it is not significantly different to the proportion in 1995.
In terms of changes at the individual benchmarks, the percentage of students performing below the Low 
benchmark has increased significantly since 2019 (by 3 percentage points), with a corresponding decrease 
in the percentage of students performing at the High benchmark.

In contrast, since 1995 there has been significant increase (of 3 percentage points) in the percentage of 
students who performed above the Advanced benchmark, with a corresponding decrease (of 4 percentage 
points) in the percentage of students who performed at the Intermediate benchmark.

7.2	
Year 8 science results within the national context
Figure 7.7 presents the mean and distribution of Year 8 science performance for each of the Australian 
jurisdictions for TIMSS 2023. Singapore is included for comparison. The jurisdictions are shown in order 
from highest to lowest mean scores.
Read Figure 7.7 in conjunction with Table 7.2, which presents multiple comparisons of science performance 
between jurisdictions and indicates whether a jurisdiction’s performance does, or does not, differ 
significantly from another’s. The mean scores ranged from 539 points in the Australian Capital Territory to 
503 points in the Northern Territory.
Students in the Australian Capital Territory and Western Australia performed significantly higher than 
students in Queensland, New South Wales, South Australia, Tasmania and the Northern Territory.
Students in Western Australia also performed significantly higher than students in Victoria.
Tasmania had the largest standard deviation in student performance (98 points), while South Australia had 
the smallest standard deviation in performance (88 points).

7.2.1	
Performance at the international benchmarks by jurisdiction
Figure 7.8 shows the percentages of students in each jurisdiction at the international benchmarks for Year 8 
science, the proportion who attained the NPS, and the percentages for Australia as a whole. Singapore and 
the international median are shown for comparison.
The highest proportions of students who attained the NPS were in the Australian Capital Territory and 
Western Australia (76%) and lowest in the Northern Territory (67%). There was a significant difference in the 
proportions of students who attained the NPS between Western Australia and 3 other jurisdictions: South 
Australia, Tasmania and New South Wales.
The Australian Capital Territory had the highest percentage of very high performers (18%). The Northern 
Territory had the lowest percentage of very high performers, at 6%. For comparison, 47% of students 
achieved the Advanced benchmark in Singapore. The percentage of very high performers was significantly 
higher in the Australian Capital Territory and Western Australia than in New South Wales, Queensland, South 
Australia and the Northern Territory. The percentage of very high performers was significantly higher in 
Victoria than in South Australia and the Northern Territory.
The Australian Capital Territory and Western Australia had the lowest percentage of very low performers; only 
8% of students did not reach the Low benchmark. The Northern Territory had 17% of very low performers. 
In Singapore, only 3% of students were below the Low benchmark. There were no significant differences 
between the jurisdictions in terms of the percentage of very low performers.

FIGURE 7.9 (continued) Trends in Year 8 science achievement within Australia, 1995–2023, by jurisdiction

140
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
The results show that there was little significant change in scores for most of the jurisdictions in science 
achievement at Year 8 over time. Victoria was the only jurisdiction to have significantly improved since 1995, 
with an increase of 23 points. New South Wales showed a significant decrease since 2019, of 25 points.
Figure 7.10 shows the proportions of students who attained the NPS, as well as the proportions of very high 
and very low performers in Year 8 science, for each Australian jurisdiction for all TIMSS cycles from 1995 
through to 2023.
There was no significant change in the NPS from 1995 to 2023 and almost no significant change from 2019 
to 2023. Only New South Wales showed a significant decrease of 10 percentage points since 2019.
Victoria was the only jurisdiction to have a significant increase in the percentage of very high performers 
since 1995 (by 7 percentage points).
South Australia was the only jurisdiction to have a significant increase in the percentage of very low 
performers since 1995 (by 5 percentage points).
There were no significant changes in the percentages of very high performers or very low performers in any 
jurisdiction since 2019.

7.3	
Year 8 science results for different demographic groups
7.3.1	
Results by gender
Figure 7.11 shows international Year 8 science achievement for male and female students. The average 
achievement is shown separately for females and males, as well as the differences between the averages. 
Differences are shown by a bar that indicates the size and direction (in favour of males or females) and 
whether the difference was statistically significant (indicated by a darkened bar). Countries are presented in 
order of the highest to lowest difference between females and males in average achievement.
Overall, there was a 3 point difference in achievement in favour of females. For 19 countries, there were no 
significant gender differences in Year 8 science achievement. Of the remaining countries, 12 had significant 
differences that favoured male students, including Australia, and 11 had significant differences favouring 
females.
Some of these differences were quite large, particularly those in favour of females. Six countries had a 
difference of over 20 points in favour of females; the Palestinian National Authority, Saudi Arabia, Oman, and 
Bahrain had a difference of over 30 points in favour of females.
Figure 7.12 shows the mean score and distribution of science achievement for male and female Year 8 
students in Australia. It shows that male students scored, on average, 13 points higher than female students. 
However, the variability in scores was greater for Year 8 males (SD = 96) than for Year 8 females (SD = 91). 
This is seen in the distribution of scores as the highest performing male students achieved at a slightly 
higher level than the highest performing female students (but there was little difference at the lower end of 
the distribution).

FIGURE 7.12 Mean scores and distribution of Year 8 science achievement within Australia, by gender
Performance at the international benchmarks by gender
Figure 7.13 presents the percentages of female and male Year 8 students at each of the international 
benchmarks for science as well as the proportions of students who attained the NPS.
The difference in the proportions of female and male students who attained the NPS was not significant.
However, as was seen in the distributions of scores, there are slightly more male than female students at 
the higher end of the scale and significantly more male students achieved the Advanced benchmark (by 5 
percentage points).
There were no significant differences in the percentages of very low performers.

Trends in science achievement by gender
Figure 7.14 shows the trends in the science achievement of Year 8 male and female students in Australia 
from 1995 to 2023. This figure shows that while the gap in mean science achievement had closed between 
2015 and 2019, the gap opened up again in 2023. This is due to a significant decrease in mean science 
achievement of 15 points since 2019 for female students. The mean science achievement for male students 
is not significantly different from 2019.

FIGURE 7.14 Trends in Year 8 science achievement within Australia, 1995–2023, by gender
Gender difference in science achievement by jurisdiction
Figure 7.15 shows the gender differences in Year 8 science by jurisdiction. All jurisdictions, except for 
the Northern Territory, had a difference in favour of males; the Australian Capital Territory had the largest 
difference of 25 points. Due to large standard errors, only Victoria and Western Australia had significant 
differences (in favour of males).
Figure 7.16 shows the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks and the proportions 
of students who attained the NPS in each jurisdiction, by gender. There were very few significant differences 
in the percentages of male and female students who achieved at each of the international benchmarks.
There were no significant differences in the proportions of male and female students who achieved the NPS 
in any jurisdiction.
In terms of very high performers, there were more male students than female students in South Australia, 
Victoria and Western Australia who achieved the Advanced benchmark.
There were no significant differences in the percentages of very low performing male and female students in 
any jurisdiction.

Chapter 7 Australia’s Year 8 science results
147
7.3.2	
Results by books in the home
This section presents Year 8 Australian students’ science achievement according to the number of books in 
the home. For more information about this variable, please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Figure 7.17 provides the percentage of students in each category and their average achievement scores. 
Forty-seven per cent of Australian Year 8 students reported having an average number of books and 18% 
reported having many books at home. The students who had many books in the home also had the highest 
levels of achievement. They scored 29 points on average higher than students who had an average number 
of books in the home, and 93 points higher than those who had a few books in the home. All differences in 
scale scores were significant.
Figure 7.17 also shows the distribution of scores in science achievement of Year 8 students for each 
category of books at home. As indicated by the standard deviation, the spread of scores is greater for those 
students with few books in the home compared to students with an average number of books or many books 
in the home.
Figure 7.18 presents the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks for science as well 
as the proportions of students who attained the NPS, by the number of books in the home.
Of those students who reported having many books in the home, 87% attained the NPS, compared to 79% of 
students with an average number of books in the home and 50% of those with only a few books in the home. 
These differences were all significant.
In terms of very high performing students, 26% of students who reported having many books in the home 
achieved the Advanced benchmark. The proportion at this highest benchmark falls away quickly, though. 
Only 14% of students in the average number of books category and just 5% in the a few books category 
attained this level of achievement. These differences were all significant.
At the other end of the scale 4% of students who reported having many books in the home and 5% with an 
average number of books in the home did not achieve even the Low international benchmark, significantly 
lower than the 22% of students who reported having a few books in the home.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume I: Student performance
7.3.3	
Results by level of parental education
Parental education has also been found to be strongly related to student achievement. This section presents 
Australian students’ science achievement according to the level of parental education. For more information 
about this variable, please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Figure 7.19 shows the mean scores and associated standard errors in science for Year 8 Australian students 
according to the highest level of education attained by either parent. As this figure shows, the mean 
score increases as the level of parental education increases. Students who had at least one parent with a 
university degree achieved a mean score a substantial 128 points higher than students whose parents did 
not complete secondary school. This was 68 points higher than the mean score for students for whom the 
highest level of parental education was completion of secondary school and 40 points higher than students 
whose parents completed a Technical and Further Education qualification. All differences were statistically 
significant.
Figure 7.19 also shows the spread of scores in science achievement for Year 8 students for the different 
parental education groups. As is indicated by the standard deviation, the spread of scores is greater for 
students whose parents did not complete secondary school (SD = 94), while the other 3 categories had a 
similar spread of scores.
Figure 7.20 presents the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks for science, as 
well as the proportions of students who attained the NPS, according to level of parental education.
Figure 7.20 shows that the proportion of students who attained the NPS increases with the level of parental 
education. The proportion of students who had at least one parent with a university degree and attained 
the NPS was 84% compared to 31% for students whose parents did not complete secondary school. All 
differences between levels of parental education were significant.
Similarly, the percentage of very high performers increases with the level of parental education; 22% of 
students who had at least one parent with a university degree achieved the Advanced benchmark, compared 
to 2% of students whose parents did not complete secondary school. All differences between levels of 
parental education were significant.
Conversely, the percentage of very low performers decreases with the level of parental education; 38% of 
students whose parents did not complete secondary school, compared to 5% of students who had at least 
one parent with a university degree. All differences between levels of parental education were significant, 
except for between students whose parents completed upper secondary education only and those whose 
parents completed post-secondary education but not university.

7.3.4	
Results by educational resources in the home
The presence or absence of educational resources in the home expresses potential advantage or 
disadvantage for students, which may reflect the ability of parents to provide materially for their children or 
indicate differences in practical and psychological support for academic achievement. This section presents 
Australian students’ science achievement according to the number of educational resources in the home. For 
more information about this variable, please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Australia recorded one of the highest proportions of students who had many resources at home and had 
42% of students in this category, similar to Cyprus, Finland, Hungary, Sweden (all 42%) and Japan (43%). 
Only Korea and Norway had higher percentages of students in this category (56% and 47%, respectively). 
Australia’s percentage of students with only a few resources at home (13%) was also quite low by 
international standards. Korea had the lowest percentage of students with only a few resources at home 
(10%), while Israel had 11% and Finland, Japan and Norway had 12%. On average, internationally, 26% of 
students had many resources at home, 45% had some resources and 28% had  few resources at home.
Figure 7.21 shows that, in Australia, Year 8 students who had many resources in the home performed at a 
significantly higher level than those who had some resources, who again performed at a significantly higher 
level than those who had few resources. This pattern held, on average, across participating countries.
Australian Year 8 students who had many resources achieved a mean score 56 points higher than those who 
had some resources, whose average achievement was 65 points higher than those with few resources at 
home. Therefore, the average achievement of those with many resources was 121 points higher than those 
with few resources.
Figure 7.21 also shows the distribution in science achievement for Australian students for each of the 
categories of the Home educational resources scale. As is indicated by the standard deviation, the spread 
of scores is smallest for those with many resources (SD = 81), while those with some resources or few 
resources at home had a similar spread of scores.
Figure 7.22 presents the percentage of Year 8 students at each of the international benchmarks for science 
as well as the proportions of students who attained the NPS for each of the categories of the Home 
educational resources scale.
The proportion of students with many resources who attained the NPS was significantly higher than students 
with some resources (by 21 percentage points) and was also significantly higher than that of students with 
few resources (by 51 percentage points). The proportion of students with some resources who attained the 
NPS was significantly higher than that of students with few resources (by 30 percentage points).

The percentage of very high performers was significantly higher for students with many resources than 
students with some resources (by 14 percentage points) and significantly higher than that for students with 
few resources (by 20 percentage points). The percentage of very high performers was also significantly 
higher for students with some resources than students with few resources (by 7 percentage points).
The percentage of very low performers was significantly lower for students with many resources than 
students with some resources (by 9 percentage points) and significantly lower than that for students with 
few resources (by 28 percentage points). The percentage of very low performers was also significantly lower 
for students with some resources than students with few resources (by 20 percentage points).

7.3.5	
Results by First Nations background
‘Supporting Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander learners to reach their full potential’ is one of the areas for 
action identified in the Alice Springs (Mparntwe) education declaration (Education Council, 2019, p.16). As 
such, educational outcomes for young First Nations Australians are a key area of interest for the education 
community. This section presents Australian students’ science achievement according to whether students 
identified as being a First Nations Australian. For more information about this variable, please refer to the 
Reader’s guide.
Seven per cent of the TIMSS sample at Year 8 identified as being a First Nations Australian. As shown in 
Figure 7.23, First Nations students attained an average score in science of 451 points. Figure 7.23 also 
presents the distribution of Year 8 achievement. As is indicated by the standard deviation, the spread of 
scores for First Nations students is slightly larger (SD = 97) than the spread of scores for non-First Nations 
students (SD = 92).

Figure 7.24 presents the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks for science as well 
as the proportions of students who attained the NPS for First Nations students.
Forty-four per cent of First Nations students achieved the NPS.
Two per cent of First Nations students were very high performers, while 31% were very low performers.
All differences with non-First Nations students were significant.
Figure 7.25 shows the trends in achievement of First Nations students from 1995 to 2023. While the general 
trend seems to be improvement over time since 1995, the (not statistically significant) drop in scores since 
2019 means that the 2023 mean score of First Nations students is not statistically significant compared 
to 1995. In addition, the apparent trend of the gap narrowing between First Nations and non-First Nations 
students between 1995 and 2015 has reversed and widened again; the 2023 gap of 76 points is similar to the 
1995 gap of 79 points.

7.3.6	
Results by language spoken at home
The frequency with which English is spoken at home is a factor that has been associated with Year 8 
science achievement in past cycles of TIMSS. Students who come from homes in which English is not 
spoken frequently have less exposure to the language of instruction and test, which can place them at 
a disadvantage. However, this variable can be difficult to interpret, as the language-spoken group is very 
diverse. While it includes those migrants to Australia who have limited English language skills at home, there 
is also a substantial proportion of students in this group who are economic immigrants to Australia, and, 
while perhaps English is not the main language at home, this is no barrier to their understanding of science.
This section presents Year 8 science achievement according to whether a language other than English was 
spoken as the main language at home. For more information about this variable, please refer to the Reader’s 
guide.
Figure 7.26 shows that 10% of students in the TIMSS Year 8 sample indicated that they did not speak English 
at home always or almost always. Students who spoke mainly English at home attained an average score in 
science of 524 points, significantly higher than students who spoke a language other than English at home 
(506 points).
Figure 7.26 also shows the distribution of scores for students by their language background. As is indicated 
by the standard deviation, the spread of scores for students who spoke a language other than English at 
home (SD = 102) was larger than that of students with an English-speaking background (SD = 93).
Figure 7.27 presents the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks for science as well 
as the proportions of students who attained the NPS by language background.
The proportion of students with an English-speaking background who attained the NPS was significantly 
higher than students who spoke a language other than English at home (by 8 percentage points).
The percentage of students with an English-speaking background who were very low performers was 
significantly lower than students who spoke a language other than English at home (by 6 percentage points).
There was no significant difference in the percentage of very high performers according to the language 
background.

7.3.7	
Results by geographic location of the school
Past cycles of TIMSS have found that students who attended schools in remote or regional areas of Australia 
are often at an educational disadvantage compared to students at schools in major cities. This section 
presents Australian students’ science achievement according to the geographic location of the school, 
reported using the 3 broad categories (major cities, regional, remote) of the Australian Bureau of Statistics 
Australian statistical geography standard remoteness structure (ABS, 2011). For more information about this 
variable, please refer to the Reader’s guide.
Figure 7.28 shows Year 8 science performance of students from schools according to the geographic 
location of the school. Students who attended schools in remote areas made up only 1% of the Year 8 TIMSS 
sample, while those who attended schools in major cities made up 74% of the sample. On average, students 
in schools in major cities scored 22 points higher than students who attended schools in regional areas, 
and 31 points higher than students in remote schools. Only the difference between students in schools in 
major cities and students in schools in regional areas was significant (due to the large standard error for the 
remote schools category).
Figure 7.28 also provides the spread of scores for science achievement by geographic location of school. 
As is indicated by the standard deviation, the spread of scores for students who attended schools in remote 
areas (SD = 91) and major cities (92 points) was similar. However, students who attended schools in regional 
areas had a greater spread than the other 2 groups (SD = 97).
Figure 7.29 shows the percentages of students at each of the international benchmarks and the proportions 
of students who attained the NPS, by geographic location of school.
The proportion of students who attended schools in major cities and attained the NPS was higher than 
students who attended regional schools (by 7 percentage points) and also higher than that of students who 
attended remote schools (by 11 percentage points). Only the difference between students in schools in 
major cities and students in schools in regional areas was significant (due to the large standard error for the 
remote schools category).
The percentage of very high performers was significantly higher for students in schools in major cities than 
in regional schools (by 4 percentage points).
There was no significant difference in the percentage of very low performers according to the geographic 
location of the school.

7.4	
Year 8 science results in the TIMSS content and cognitive domains
As noted in Chapter 5, the TIMSS science assessment can be described in terms of content and cognitive 
domains. The content domains outline the subject matter to be assessed and include Biology, Chemistry, 
Physics and Earth science at Year 8. The cognitive domains detail the thinking processes that students 
need to use. These domains are Knowing, Applying and Reasoning. Each item is associated with a single 
content domain and a single cognitive domain. This allows student performance to be described in terms of 
achievement in each of the domains.
To allow comparisons of student achievement across the domains, the content and cognitive achievement 
scales at each year level were constructed to have the same average level of difficulty. The tables in this 
section present the average achievement in each of the Year 8 science content and cognitive domains for 
Australia as a whole, for each of the Australian jurisdictions, for males and females, and for First Nations 
students. These tables also show trends for Australia in the content and cognitive domains since 2007.
7.4.1	
Science content domains: Biology, Chemistry, Physics, and 
Earth science
Table 7.3 provides the Year 8 achievement scores in the science content domains for Australia, the 
jurisdictions, by gender and by First Nations background.
Australian Year 8 students performed relatively better in Physics and Earth science and relatively less well in 
Biology and Chemistry. These differences were statistically significant for Australia as a whole.
The relatively poorer performance for Biology and relatively stronger performance for Physics held for all 
jurisdictions, for male and female students and by First Nations background (although not all differences 
were statistically significant due to larger standard errors).
However, for Chemistry, both female and First Nations students showed very little variation from the overall 
science score.
Similarly, for Earth science, female students’ performance was not significantly different to that of science 
overall.
Male students performed significantly higher than female students on all domains except for Chemistry.
Non-First Nations students performed significantly higher than First Nations students in all content domains.
Table 7.4 shows the trends in achievement for the content domains for Australia as a whole.
Performance in Biology has dropped by a significant 18 points compared to 2019, whereas performance in 
Chemistry, Physics and Earth science is not significantly different to 2019.

7.4.2	
Science cognitive domains: Knowing, Applying, and Reasoning
Table 7.5 presents the Year 8 achievement scores in the science cognitive domains for Australia, the 
jurisdictions, gender and First Nations background.
Australian Year 8 students performed relatively better in the Reasoning cognitive domain and relatively less 
well in Knowing. These differences were statistically significant for Australia as a whole.
However, the relative performance across the domains was not statistically significant in all jurisdictions due 
to large standard errors – despite fairly similar patterns of relative performance across the domains.
The pattern of relative performance seen for Australia was mirrored for female students but not for male 
students, who did not have any significantly different performance across the domains.
Interestingly, given the larger relative performance in Reasoning for female students but no such difference 
for male students, there was no significant difference in performance in Reasoning between male and female 
students (whereas male students outperformed female students in Knowing, Applying and overall science).
Table 7.6 shows the trends in achievement for the cognitive domains for Australia as a whole. The table 
indicates significant decline in Applying and Reasoning since 2019 but not for Knowing.

7.5	
Environmental awareness
TIMSS 2023 includes an expanded focus on environmental awareness, assessing both knowledge and 
attitudes. By assessing these, TIMSS 2023 provides a more comprehensive understanding of students’ 
environmental literacy, and thus their ability to make informed decisions about sustainability and actively 
participating in addressing environmental challenges.
This section covers Year 8 students’ achievement on the Environmental knowledge subscale, as well as their 
attitudes towards the preservation of the environment.
7.5.1	
Environmental knowledge: Results within the international context
To understand what students around the world know about environmental issues, the Environmental 
knowledge subscale was constructed based on the achievement items that covered environmental science 
in the TIMSS 2023 assessment. At Year 8, the Environmental knowledge subscale included 58 items drawn 
from the Biology and Earth science subdomains. The range of items addressed a variety of environmental 
topics, from local issues such as water pollution to global issues such as climate change and its impact.
Australian Year 8 students achieved a mean score of 519 points in Environmental knowledge. This 
performance was lower than students in 7 countries (Singapore, Chinese Taipei, Japan, Korea, Finland, 
Sweden and Hong Kong,); not significantly different to students in 7 countries (England, Ireland, Türkiye, 
Portugal, Hungary, Czech Republic and the United States,) and higher than students in 27 countries (including 
Malta, France, Israel, Qatar and the United Arab Emirates).
Figure 7.30 provides the mean scores achieved by students in each country, the standard error and 
confidence interval of each mean and the standard deviation (an indication in the variability of individual 
scores around the mean). Countries are positioned from highest to lowest level of achievement; however, 
this is not a simple ranking, as the differences between countries may not be statistically significant. 
The shading indicates whether the score for a given country was significantly different to Australia’s. To 
determine whether or not differences between other countries were statistically significant, please refer to 
the TIMSS 2023 international results in mathematics and science (von Davier et al., 2024).

Chapter 7 Australia’s Year 8 science results
159
Australia’s mean score of 519 points in Environmental knowledge was not significantly different to the 
overall score in science of 520 points. However, many countries did show a difference in achievement 
in Environmental knowledge compared to their overall score in science. Ten of the countries had relative 
achievement in Environmental knowledge that was lower than their overall score in science (including 
Singapore, Chinese Taipei and Japan); while 17 countries had relative achievement in Environmental 
knowledge that was higher than their overall score in science (including Sweden, Portugal and Norway).

7.5.2	
Environmental knowledge: Results within the national context
Figure 7.31 presents the mean and distribution of Year 8 students’ achievement in Environmental knowledge 
performance for each of the Australian jurisdictions, gender and First Nations background. Figure 7.31 is 
supported by Table 7.7, which presents multiple comparisons of achievement in Environmental knowledge 
between the jurisdictions and indicates whether a jurisdiction’s performance does, or does not, differ 
significantly from another’s.
The mean scores in Environmental knowledge for the jurisdictions, gender, and First Nations background did 
not differ significantly from the mean score on overall science. Therefore, the pattern of achievement was 
similar for Environmental knowledge as for overall science.
Comparing performance across the jurisdictions, the Australian Capital Territory and Western Australia 
performed significantly higher than students in Queensland, New South Wales, South Australia, Tasmania 
and the Northern Territory. Students in Western Australia also performed significantly higher than students in 
Victoria.
In terms of performance by gender, male students scored on average 19 points higher than female students 
on Environmental knowledge.
In terms of performance by First Nations background, there was an 80-point difference between First Nations 
and non-First Nations students on Environmental knowledge.

7.5.3	
Students value environmental preservation
TIMSS 2023 collected data on how strongly students valued environmental preservation. Students were 
asked to indicate their level of agreement (agree a lot, agree a little, disagree a little or disagree a lot) with 
each of the following 6 statements:
	
z I care about the protection of plants and animals.
	
z It makes me sad when nature is destroyed by humans.
	
z I enjoy finding out what kinds of plants and animals live in my surrounding area.
	
z I enjoy being in nature (e.g. parks, beaches, the bush, deserts).
	
z Protecting nature is more important than economic growth.
	
z Addressing climate change should be a high priority.
Responses to these statements were combined to create the Students value environmental preservation 
scale.
At Year 8, students who very strongly value environmental preservation had a score on the scale of at least 
10.2, which corresponds to their agreeing a lot with 3 of the statements and agreeing a little with the other 3, 
on average. Students who somewhat value environmental preservation had a score that was no higher than 
7.8, which corresponds to their disagreeing a little with half of the 6 statements and agreeing a little with the 
remaining half. All other students strongly valued environmental preservation.
Figure 7.32 shows the percentages of Year 8 students in each of the 3 categories of the Students value 
environmental preservation scale, and their associated scores in Environmental knowledge in each category.

FIGURE 7.32 The Students value environmental preservation scale and Year 8 student achievement in Environmental

Thirty-eight per cent of Australian Year 8 students said that they very strongly value environmental 
preservation, and 45% reported that they strongly value environmental preservation, with only 17% indicating 
that they somewhat value environmental preservation. Internationally on average across countries, 48% 
of students said that they very strongly value environmental preservation, and 41% reported that they 
strongly value environmental preservation, and only 12% indicated that they somewhat value environmental 
preservation.
There was a positive relationship between valuing environmental preservation and average Environmental 
knowledge achievement. Australian Year 8 students who very strongly value environmental preservation 
scored around 43 points higher on Environmental knowledge than students who only somewhat value 
environmental preservation.

APPENDICES
Appendix A: TIMSS methods and procedures
To assist readers to understand the scope and operations of TIMSS, a brief account of some of its 
procedures is provided in this appendix. A thorough account is available in the TIMSS 2023 technical report 
(2023). As most of the operational procedures have both international and national components, this 
appendix provides details specific to Australia, where appropriate. 
Operationalisation of TIMSS
Procedures for administering the test were determined by the TIMSS International Study Center so that 
data from all students from all schools in all countries could be considered equivalent. These were 
operationalised by National Centres in each country, such as ACER in Australia. School coordinators, 
nominated by the principal of each participating school, assisted the National Centre with the management 
of TIMSS within the school, including administering the school questionnaire and teacher questionnaire. The 
actual tests and student questionnaires were administered by a test administrator, who, in most cases, was 
a teacher from the school. The test administrator followed strict guidelines and was required to complete 
a report about any situation that deviated from these guidelines. A National Quality Control Observer 
(employed by the National Centre) visited 10% of schools to observe the test administration. An International 
Quality Control Observer (employed by the IEA) visited a further 15 schools at each year level as well as 
examining the operations of the National Centre.
Detailed information about the operations of TIMSS 2023 is available in Chapter 4 of the TIMSS 2023 
technical report (2023).
Sampling
The TIMSS 2023 assessment was administered to carefully drawn random samples of students from the 
target population in each country. Because the accuracy of the TIMSS results depends on the quality of the 
national samples, the TIMSS sampling experts worked with participating countries on all phases of sampling 
to ensure efficient sampling design and implementation.
National Centre staff were trained in how to select the school and student samples, and in how to use the 
sampling software provided by the IEA. Staff from RTI International (the TIMSS 2023 sampling partner) 
and the IEA reviewed the national sampling plans, sampling data, sampling frames and sample selections. 
The sampling documentation was used by the TIMSS International Study Center (in consultation with RTI 
International, the IEA and the sampling referee) to evaluate the quality of the samples.

164
Appendices
Internationally, the target populations of students are defined as follows:
	
z Grade 4: all students enrolled in the grade that represents 4 years of schooling counting from the first year 
of Level 1 of the International Standard Classification of Education (ISCED), providing the mean age at the 
time of testing is at least 9.5 years
	
z Grade 8: all students enrolled in the grade that represents 8 years of schooling counting from the first year 
of ISCED Level 1, providing the mean age at the time of testing is at least 13.5 years.
All students enrolled in the target grade, regardless of their age, belong to the international target population 
and should be eligible to participate in TIMSS. Where the national target population differed from the 
international target population, this was annotated in the international reports. In Australia, the target 
populations are Year 4 students and Year 8 students.
Within the target population, countries could define a population that excluded a small percentage (no more 
than 5%) of certain kinds of schools or students that would be very difficult or resource intensive to test 
(for example, schools for students with special needs or schools that were very small or located in remote 
rural areas). In Australia, school-level exclusions included very small schools (less than 5 students in the 
target year level), non-mainstream schools (such as schools for students with special needs) and very 
remote schools. Within-school exclusions consisted of students with intellectual disabilities, students with 
functional disabilities and non-native language speakers (with less than one year of exposure to English). 
Table A.1 provides the exclusion rates in Australia.

The basic design of the sample used in TIMSS 2023 was a 2-stage stratified cluster design. The first stage 
sampled schools, and the second stage sampled intact classrooms from the target year level in the sampled 
schools. Schools were selected with probability proportional to size, and classrooms with equal probabilities. 
Most countries sampled 150 schools and one or two intact classrooms from each school. This approach 
was designed to yield a representative sample of at least 4,000 students in each country. For information 
about this approach to sampling, please refer to Chapter 3 of the TIMSS 2023 technical report (2023).
In Australia, a larger sample of schools and students participated in TIMSS to produce reliable estimates 
representative of each of the Australian jurisdictions, since a random sample proportionate to jurisdiction 
populations would not yield enough students in each of the jurisdictions to give a result of sufficient 
precision.
At the school level in Australia, the intended sample was 290 schools at Year 4 and Year 8. To produce the 
representative sample, this sample was stratified in the following manner: 
	
z explicit stratification (where a separate sample was drawn for each stratum) – by jurisdiction
	
z implicit stratification (where the schools were sorted according to the stratification variables within each 
of the explicit strata) – by geographic location (major cities, regional, remote), school type (Catholic, 
government, independent) and socioeconomic index (low socioeconomic status, high socioeconomic 
status).
Table A.2 shows the designed school sample and the distribution of schools across the jurisdictions. 
Following sampling, some schools were withdrawn from the sample, either because they were ineligible 
(lacked students from the target population) or because all of their students fell into an exclusion category. 
In addition, some schools that were unable to participate for reasons other than ineligibility or exclusion were 
replaced by schools that had been identified as suitable replacements during the sampling process. Where 
a school was withdrawn too late for replacement, they were recorded as a ‘refusal’. Table A.2 summarises 
these changes to the sample.

Class sampling was undertaken after school sampling. The usual process was to sample only one 
mathematics classroom for each school. However, in cases where the classes were small (such as 
composite classes), at least 2 classes were sampled to allow the total number of students to more closely 
approximate the average class size. In addition, in Australia, where schools used streaming or tracking 
to allocate students to classes, 2 classes were sampled in order to balance out the academic abilities of 
selected students.
Within-school exclusions of students were allowed where disability or language barriers prevented the 
students’ full participation in TIMSS 2023. These exclusions were either of full classes (where any such class 
comprised students with special needs) or of individual students within sampled classes.
Table A.3 shows the student sample sizes achieved, as well as the numbers of excluded, absent and 
withdrawn students (withdrawn students were students that had left the school between the sampling of the 
class and the assessment date).

To ensure accurate and unbiased data, the TIMSS & PIRLS International Study Center set minimum 
participation rates of 85% of sampled schools and 85% cent of sampled students (or a combined school 
and student participation rate of 75%). Non-participating sampled schools could be replaced by replacement 
schools that had been matched according to strata and size. However, countries that achieved these 
requirements only through replacement schools are annotated in the international reports. Countries that had 
less than 50% of sampled schools participate are segregated in the international reports. Table A.4 shows 
that Australia achieved the minimum participation rate for both Population 1 (Year 4) and Population 2 
(Year 8).
